Correspondence respecting the Condition of Protestants 
in Turkey, 


No. 1. 


Fiscount Palmerston (o Viscount Ponsonby. 
(No. 21.) 
My Lord, Foreign Office, February 8, 181%. 

I TRANSMIT to your Excellency a copy ofa letter from Sir Thomas B aring, 
from which it would appear that the Society for Promoting Christianity among 
the Jews having been made acquainted in 1837 with the failure of your Excel- 
leney’s endeavours to obtain the formal sanction of the Porte to the erection of 
a Protestant chapel at Jerusalem, adopted a suggestion which I made to them 
at the time, and acquired indirectly the means of celebrating religious worship 
at Jerusalem without exposing themselves to the interference of the Turkish 
Authorities. The Society now wish that advantage should be taken of the pre- 
sent state of affairs in the Levant to obtain from the Porte a formal assent to 
the establishment of a Protestant chapel at Jerusalem, and to the registration 
* such chapel in the name of the Rey. J. Nicolayson, on behalf of the 
nociety. 

There can be no doubt that a compliance by the Porte with this request 
would alford yery great pleasure to the public in this country, for there is a 
strong and general feeling here which is becoming daily more and more preva- 
lent, that considering the deep obligations conferred upon the Porte by Christian 
Powers during the last twelve months, the Porte ought to permit the Christian 
worship to be openly performed in the city of Jerusalem. 

I leave it to your Hxeellency’s judgment and diseretion to take such steps 
as you may think best caleulated for the accomplishment of this object, but it 
is a matter in which Her Majesty's Government take a deep interest, and in 
which they are extremely anxious to succeed, 

| Tam, &e. 
(Signed) PALMERSTON, 


Inelosure in No, 1. 
Sir. T. Baring to Vascount Palmerston, 


My Lord, Stratton Park, Winchester, February 1, 1841. 

AS President of the London Society for Promoting Christianity amongst 
the Jews, | have been requested hy its Managers to again address your Lord- 
ship upon the subject of the Episcopal Chapel at Jerusalem, 

If not in the recollection of your Lordship, it cannot fail to be still in the 
vrateful remembrance of the whole body of the Society that upon an application 
made to your Lordship in the year 1837 to send out instructions to the British 
Resident at the Egyptian Court to obtain permission for the erection of a chapel 
and suitable buildings for the missionaries. it met with the most prompt 
and cordial acquiescence, and a despatch was immediat ely forwarded to Colonel 
Campbell, the then Consul-General at Alexandria, directing him to apply in the 
name of His Britannic Majesty’s Government for the permission required 
Your Lordship was at the same time pleased to inform me as President that 
instructions had likewise been transmitted to the British Ambassador at Con- 
stantinople to support the views of the Socicty in case of any (difficulty arising: 
from that quarter.

No. 3. 


The Chevalier Bunsen to Visegunt Palmerston. 


Londres; ce 15 Juillet, 1811. 
Lis Soussigne, Envoye Extraordinaire dle Sa Majeste oe Se Eeueees in 
miesion spécialé prés In Cour Hritannique, & été hee Me ae abn es gt : f 
muitre, de faire & ga Selgneurie le Vicomte AuMeretOn; poe ia 
Majesté Britannique pour les Affaires Etrangeres, la commur can A 
Le Roi, ma par Ta position actuelle, nouvelle et unique | ; 


3 


Puissances Chrétiennes vis-i-vis de la Porte Ottomanne, désirant contribuer de 
sa part, de la maniére la plus efficace et la plus durable, 4 l’amelioration perma- 
nente du sort des Chrétiens dans I'Mmpire Ture, et soulaitant en méme temps 
de yoir se resserrer toujours plus étroitement les hens d'amitié et de confiance 
entre les deux Couronnes et les nations soumises a leur sceptre, a résolu d'in- 
viter, par l’envoi d'une mission spéciale, le Gouvernement Britannique 4 une 
union intime des deux Puissances, ayant un but tout pacifique, et un objet 
lié intimement avec les intéréts les plus chers et les plus sacrés des deux nations 
et de Vhumanité en entier, 

Ce but est principalement d’obtenir de la Porte Ottomane, par le moyen 
de négociations communes # Constantinople, la reconnaissance de I'Eglise Pro- 
testante en Turquie, comme d'une corporation religieuse, a instar des cor- 
porations existantes des Latins, des Grecs, des Arméniens, et d'autres sem- 
blables, de maniére que les Chrétiens, sujets de la Porte et résidens, appartenant 
aux Eglises nationales des deux pays, solent autorisés & se constituer partout 
en communautés, et agir conformément 4 ce titre, cest & dire, 4 aequérir des 
proprictés comme personnes morales, a batir des églises, & avoir des évéques 
et autres fonetionnaires ecclésiastiques, A recevoir ceux qui pourraient venir 
se jomndre a eux, ct en général de faire toutes les actions légales d'une sociéte 
reconnue. 

‘ette reconnaissance mettrait donc les membres des deux Eglises natio- 
nales en Turquie simplement au pied des communautés Chrétiennes, qui, 
constituées em corps de nation, y jouissent déjA d'une telle reconnaissance 
légale: parité de position, également réclamée, dans le moment actuel, par la 
position politique et religieuse des deux grandes Puissances Protestantes en 
Europe, et par les dispositions générales du Hatti-Schérif de Gulhané et les 
arrangemens récens pour les communautés Chretiennes existantes en Palestine 
eb en Syrie. 

La Palestine, au centre de laquelle I'éclise nationale de Ll’ Angleterre 
possede déji un Ctablissement religieux naissant, est objet principal des soins 
de Sa Majesté dans l'execution du projet & laquelle elle invite le Gouvernement 
Britannique. 

Mais il n'est pas du tout dans les intentions de Sa Majesté le Roi, de pro- 
poser en aucune manitre des démarches ayant pour but de procurer 4 la 
communauté Protestante une part queleonque aux ¢tablissemens des autres 
éclises Chretiennes, et aux priviléges locaux, en possession desquels celles-ci se 
trouvent, comme nommeément au Sint Sépulchre et aux autres lieux saints de 
la Palestine. 

A efté de cette reconnaissance de In communauté Protestante, comme 
telle, Sa Majesté désire se joindre ¢troitement 4 l’Angleterre, pour assurer nux 
sujets des deux Couronnes en général, sans aucune distinction religieux, la 
veritable jouissanee de cette protection des personnes et des proprictés, que le 
Hatti-Schéril de Gulliané promet également 4 tous les habitans de ja Turquie, et 
4 leur procurer toutes les facilites pour résider et se fixer dans le pays. 

Sa Majesté, en laissant, avee une confiance ilimitée, au Gouvernement 
Britannique le choix des moyens quil jugera les plus convenables pour 
atteindre ces objets, declare d’avance qu’elle se joindra volontiers 4 toutes les 
mesures et démarches qui puissent seconder la négocintion, et qui soient. propres 
A dlargir ct affermir In base de Ia protection des intérdts Chrétiens dans 
"Empire Ottoman, et par cela méme uider la cause de la véritable civilisation 
et le vrai bien-¢ire de lhumanite, 

Le principe d'une telle coopération intime admis, Sa Majesteé attendra du 
Gouvernement Britannique li communication des instructions qu'il donners & 
ce sujet &l’Ambassade Anglais prés ln Porte Ottomane, pour faire parvenir 
des ordres analogues i son Ministre i Constantinople, 

Sa Majesté se plait A espérer, que la proposition que par Vorgane du 
Soussigné elle vient de faire au Gouyeriement Britannique, proposition qui 
nimplique aucune demande de privildges exclusifs ou de prérogatives, soit 
politiques ou religicuses, qui ne blesse les intéréts légitimes d’aucune autre 
Puissance et d'aucune autre confession Chrétienne, ct qui promet les résultats 
les plus satisfaisans pour le développement paisible de la civilisation et pour les 
intéréts réunis de la science, de Vindustrie et du commerce, enfin pour le main- 
tien de la paix générale, condition de la jouissance de tous ces bienfaits, trouvera 
auprés du Gouvernement de Sa Majeste Britanpique un accueil favorable, 


4 


conforme aux sentimens de désintéressement, d'amitié et de confiance qui |'ont 
inspirée. 

F Le Soussigné s’estime heureux d’avoir été choisi par le Roi son auguste 
maitre, 4 étre l’organe d'une telle mission de paix et de confiance auprés de Sa 
Seigneurie le Vicomte Palmerston, au moment méme ot Vauyre de la solution 
pacifique de la question Turque et de la pacification de l'Europe, basée sur le 
Traité A jamais mémorable du 15 Juillet, vient d'étre couronnée d'un suceeés 


complet, et il saisit, &c. . | 
(Signe) BUNSEN. 


No. 4. 


Viscount Ponsonby to Viscount Palmerston—(Keceived July 30.) 
(No. 229.) 
My Lord, Therapia, July 11, 1841, 

THE Porte cannot grant leave to build a church. The Porte will make 
no objection to the reparation of any building, large or small, that can be sup- 
posed to have been a church at any time. 

The late arrangements made for Syria will probably facilitate anything 
Mr. Nieolayson may do at Jerusalem, in pursuance of the project for raising a 
church there, He may, I believe, begin by having a chapel in a house, the pro- 
perty of a Christian, and in a short time it may be allowed to be considered as 
a sacred building, which may be repaired, In some such way Mr, Nicolayson 
may, with a little patience, and prudence, and care to avoid openly violating 
the law of this country, establish a church. He eannot obtain permission to 
violate the law in open, barefaced guise. It has been said that the English 
Government is ready to allow a mosque to be raised in London; but that would 
not he in violation of law, or Parliament might make a law to authorise it. 
Here the ease is wholly different. The raising a new chureh 1s contrary to the 
law, and the Sultan himself ought not, and cannot, break a law intimately eon- 
nected with the religion of which he is the chief Pontiff It is denied that 
Mehemed Ali granted permission to build a new chureh; but were it true, it 
would he no reason for the Porte to make a similar grant, 

The firman desired for Mr. Nicolayson 1s totally unattainable. 1 do not 
see how the Porte can be foreed to grant such demands as are contained in the 
instructions for the guidance of Mr. Nicolayson. 

The promotion of our religion in.this country would bea great good, and 
I fear the use of violent means would be anything rather than favourable to the 
attainment of that desirable end, If Mr, Nicolayson will be content to have a 
place of worship defacto, he shall be able inn short time to make it one 
de jure. 

| I have, &e. 
(Signed) PONSONBY.

Inclosure in No. 6. 
M, Pisani to Viscount Ponsonby. 


My dy Me ar Pera, July 25, 1841. 
4G leave to inform your ency that having, agre ; 

express commands, applied again to the Porte for roca vagal il 
church at Jerusalem or the use of Protestants, I found the objection we have 
already met with on this score has lost nothing of its strength. | 

flis Excellency Rifaat Pasha says that, with all their good dispositions te 
do what may be agreeable to the Brifish Government, the Porte cannot comply 
with a demand that is contrary to the Turkish law. 2 

Tf some churches, says Rifaat Pasha, have been built in the Turkish 
empire, it was merely for the wants of the Christian Rayahs, who would not 
remain in Turkey if they were not allowed to have churches, It was on that 
consideration that permission was originally granted to build churches: and it 
is still for the same reason that permission is given to repair such of them as 
really want repairing, But as for ission to erect. a new temple at Jerusa- 
lem, it is out of the question, I therefore beg his Excellency Lord Ponsonby 
to accept our excuses for not granting the permission applied for as above, 

_ Thave, &c. 
(Signed) F, PISANI.

ea a Therapia, September 15, 1841. 

. * obedience to your instructions, I have had communication with hj 
Exel ancy the Prussian Envoy, on the subject of establishing Protestant

confidence, that the wish so often expressed by Her Majesty's Government, for 
permission to build a ehurch at Jerusalem is not unlikely to be ratified 
Some delay must at any rate take place before the question can be ne ciel 
with much prospect of success in the Council, but I know that .a very influential 
person is at present occupied in bringing matters to bear upon the members of 
that hody, so as toallow me to express a confident hope of its final succes 

' he Prussian Chargé d’Affaires gave me a despatch to read from his 
Government in which His Prussian Majesty’s earnest desire for the success of 
this measure is expressed, and M, de Wagner is instructed to give his assistance 
to Her Majesty’s Embassy for its final accomplishment, 

[ observe in this despatch that the Prussian Government lias laid aside for 
the moment, the idea of establishing a Protestant community duly acknowledged 
by the Porte, as is the case with the Catholies and Greeks, and has adopted 
the principle advanced by Her Majesty's Government of first procuring the 
steno - church at Jerusalem, and making that a basis for any future

TLER Majesty’s Government have had under their consideration several 
despathees which have recently been received at this office from Her Majesty’s 
Consul-Goneral in Syria, as well as from Her Majesty’s Consul at Jerusalem 
respecting the conduct of the Turkish authorities at Jerusalem and Beyrout, 
with regard to the Protestant church at Jerusalem, the erection of which after 
having been for some time tacitly permitted by the Turkish authorities has at 
length been abruptly and somewhat arbitrarily stopped, ats 
Although that building had certainly been commenced without the express 
authority of the Porte, which had always declined granting a formal permission 
tor that object, yet, as it had been stated to Her 4 njesty’s Government by Her 
Majesty’s Ambassador at Constantinople (as appears from a despatch dated 
September 15, 1841, No, 285), that he had reason to suppose that, provided the 
fabric should he modest and unostentatious in appearance and should form part 
of the Consular residence, no obstruction would be thrown by the Turkish autho- 
rities in the wiy of its erection ; and as that building had not for many months 
been opposed or obstructed, Her Majesty's Government had certainly hoped 
that the further prosecution of it might and would have been allowed, . 
Her Majesty's Government still entertain a hope that, on a temperate 
representation of their wishes being made to that effect, the Turkish Govern

Sir Stratford Canning to the Earl of Aberdeen..—{ Received May 7.) 
(No. 82.) 


My Lord, Constantinople, April 18, 1843. 
SOME time before the receipt of your Lordship’s instruction, No, 41 of 
this year, I had endeavoured by confidential means to obtain the revocation of 
the order by which the construction of the Protestant Emseopal church at 
Jerusalem has been suspended, 1 had so far succeeded as to receive an 
assurance that the Sultan would not object to issue the desired firman, provided 
the consent of the Mufti were obtained, and also a promise that the Mufti 
should be requested to give his consent; but since the opinion of Her Majesty's 
Government respecting the affairs of Servia has been made known, | have 
found it irinosstble to make any further progress in the affair. No refusal has 
heen yet declared; but my endeavours to procure a satisfactory result are 
silently evaded, and I can only cherish a faint hope that the communication of 
your Lordship’s instructions to Sarim Effendi, whenever a convenient oppor- 
tunity may oceur, will be attended with more effect, 
Thave, &e, 
(Signed) STRATFORD CANNING.

No. 14, 


Sir Stratford Canning to the Earl of Aberdeen.—{ Received May 23.) 
(No. 93.) 


My Lord, Constantinople, May 1, 1843. 
The Prussian Chargé d’Affaires has communicated to me a despatch from 
M. de Bulow respecting the church at Jerusalem, and expressive of a wish to 
obtain permission from the Porte for the completion of that edifice, I told 
Count de Portalés that I had similar instructions from your Lordship, I ex- 
plained to him the course which I had hitherto pursued, and declared my 
readiness to co-operate with him for the accomplishment of the object. in 
question. He said, with renee to taking any simultaneous step, that he should 
preter waiting for the arrival of M. le Cog, who was already on the road, and 
might be expected within a fow days, 


As soon as that minister arrives I shall not fail to communicate with him 
upon the subject. 

I sent Mr, Alison to Sarim Effendi again this morning. The conversa- 
tion was confidential; I wish it were in m power to add that it was satisfactory. 
According to present appearances, it would seem that a firman is out of the 
uestion—that a private prmaesion from the Porte to go on with the building 
is not likely to be yielded without much opposition, and that little chance 
exists of obtaining more than a local connivance at the covering in of the walls, 
which might, by skilful and patient management, be eventually improved into a 
completion of the building, 

I have, &e. 
(Signed) STRATFORD CANNING.

No. 16. 
The Chevalier Bunsen to the Earl of Aberdeen.—( Received August 19.) 


My Lord, London, Auguat 17, 1843, 


IN reference to the verbal communications I had the honour of making to 

our Lordship some days ago, respecting the present state of the Protestant 
Bishopric at Jerusalem, and of the buildings begun, or about to be erected, at 
that place, I beg to submit to you Bi copies of the plans which have been 


10 


drawn up lately by the architect sent to Jerusalem by the Society for the 
Conversion of the Jews. 

These plans have been made in strict adherence to the principle laid down 
from the beginning, viz., that the church should form a part of the dwelling- 
houses erected on the premises, and so as not to be too conspicuous to the 
public. Your Lordship will observe, that the church, not considerably higher 
than the surrounding houses, is not exposed to the public view, but concealed 
from the eye on all sides, Its structure will be in the simplest style of a 
basilica, without those towers and cupolas which the former arelitect had 
planned, ‘This church is constructed for holding at the utmost three hundred 
people, a size which will be found very moderate, if it is considered that, even 
under the present unfavourable circumstances, the congregation has frequently 
amounted to fifty. re ; 

The plan further hag been made on the principle, that the official resi- 
dences of the two respective Consuls should form part of the premises. The 
Consular houses, each a square of fifty feet, constitute the entrance to the 
quadrangle from the street, opposite to the church, The church thus appears 
such as it is intended to be, that is to say, destined for the use of the Consular 
Agents, and the Protestant Ministers, and other persons of their respective 
nations under their protection, and forming, as it were, a common part of their 
establishment. | 

At present the church, which is about four feet above ground, and a part 
of one wing of the quadrangle, are all that are in existence; but directions 
will be given to construct. the buildings round the quadrangle and the Bishop's 
residence, a8 soon as the detailed plans will have been made out, The 
architect already mentioned, lately returned by Berlin from Jerusalem, is 
actively engaged in completing them, to be executed by an assistant of his 
left on the spot. His Majesty’s Consul has orders to superintend such of the 
buildings as are to be erected by the money collected last year in the Pro- 
testant Churches of Prussia for this purpose, viz. the residence for the Prussian 
Consul, (the Society erecting that Me the English Consul,) the schools, (left 
wing of the quadrangle,) and the hospice for visitors, behind the Bishop's 
residence, 3 

Under these circumstances it seems most urgent, that the Porte should 
do away by her supreme authority that unwarrantable measure by which, six 
months ago, the building of the church was stopped : & measure which, as it 
appears from evidence collected on the spot, did not even originate at Constan- 
tinople, but at Beyrout and Jerusalem, in local intrigues, and in the inypression 
of the Pasha of the time, that the British Consul lad disavowed all knowledge 
of the church and its building, The plea of the ground being inalienable 
yakoof is untenable, according to the documents of the purchase, and the 
acquisition 18 a legally good one. | 

As to the building upon that ground of a church, to be erected as part of 
what may be termed Consular premises, it seems impossible that the Ottoman 
Porte should seriously refuse its firman for sanctioning tt. ; 

On these considerations I bee to submit to your Lordship the propriety 
of communicating by the next courier, one of the copies of the plan in ques~ 
tion to the British Ambassador at Constantinople, in order to assist his 
Excellency in his efforts to remove the obstacles so unexpectedly thrown in our 
way, His Majesty’s Envoy is instructed to act in this negetiation in strict 
conformity to the advice and wishes of the British Ambassador, and not 
without the same. 

I have, &c. 
(Signed) BUNSEN. 


—“ | eee 


No, 16. 
The Earl of Aberdeen to the Chevalier Bunsen. 


M, le Chevalier, Foreign Office, August 26, 1849. 

| HAVE had the honour to receive your letter of the 17th instant, inclosing 
two copies of the plans for a church and consular buildings, which have been 
drawn up lately by the architect sent to Jerusalem by the Society for the Con- 


a 


1} 


version of the Jews, and requesting that Her Majesty's Ambassador at the 
Porte might be instructed to co-operate with the Prussian Minister in his 
efforts to remove the obstacles which the Porte has thrown in the way of the 
erection of the buildings above referred to. 

In reply I have to inform you that it would appear to Her Majesty's 
Government objectionable to delegate to any private association, however re- 
speetable, the task of providing an official residence for the representative of 
the British Government at Jerusalem. And although Her Majesty’s Govern- 
ment do not mean to say, that if the Society for the Conversion of the Jews 
are able of themselves, or with the co-operation of the Prussian Minister, to 
obtain the necessary sanction from the Ottoman authorities for the erection of 
the buildings in question, Her Majesty's Government would not be ready 
to hire a portion of such buildings as a consular residence; yet they cannot 
give any positive pledge even in this respect, nor can they consent to identify 
tissorclyes with the proceedings of the Society. 

At the same time the connection of the church with the consular residence 
appears to offer the best and only prospect of being enabled to erect the edifice 
without the danger of vexatious obstruction, 

I am, &e, 
(Signed) ABERDEEN,

No, 18. 


The Earl of Aberdeen to Sir Stratford Canning. 
(No, 125.) 


Sir, Foreign Office, October 4, 1843, 

WITH reference to previous correspondence on the subject of the Pro- 
testant chureh already hegun at Jerusalem, and the recent, suspension of its 
further erection hy the Turkish authorities at that place, [ have to inform your 
Excellency that I lave received information of a very posilive nature from the 
Prussian Minister at this Court, to the effect that the allegation put forward by 
the Turkish authorities that the ground on which the building was carrying on 
was vakoof, is unfounded, 

It appears that during the recent possession of Jerusalem by the Egyptians, 
an Armenian bought that ground as an alienable vakoof, haying originally he- 
longed to a church of Jacobites ; and that he resold it openly and, as permitted 
by law, to Mr. Nicolayson, , 

Mr. Nicolayson, [ understand, will proceed to Constantinople with all the 
papers necessary to prove this fact. | 

It is faaniled to surround and coneeal the church by buildings which will 
form in part the Prussian Consulate; the British Consular residence being also 
al heg to those buildings on one side, 

‘hus the church will have the appearance of a Consular chapel, and having 
this character, Her Majesty's Government are unwilling to suppose that the 
Turkish Government will offer any further opposition to its erection; on the 
contrary, they trust that the desired firman will be granted for its continuation, 
in conformity with the Turkish principle which has always been acted on, that 


= 


I2 


where a Christian church has already been, a Christian church may agam he 
built, | 

Now it appears certain that a church of Jacobites did at one time occupy 
in part the spot on which it is now proposed to erect the Christian chapel 
already commenced, In fact, the old church is still partially im existence in 
the shape of a now deserted mosque, into which it was formerly converted. 

Your Excellency, after haying further satisfied yourself of the correetness 
of these allegations by communication with Mr. Nicolayson on his arrival at 
Constantinople, will lay these circumstances before the Porte, and aren urge 
them to give their consent to the resumption of the suspended buildings, by 
issuing a firman to that effect, and despatching the necessary instruction for 
that purpose to their authorities at Jerusalem. 


Tam, &e. 
(Signed) ABERDEEN. 


No. 19. 
Sir Stratford Canning to the Earl of Aberdeen.—(Received December 24.) 


(No. 255.) 
My Lord, Constantinople, December 1, 1843, 

THE receipt of your DLordship’s instructions respecting the church at 
Jerusalem, the Prussian Minister's communication of similar instructions 
addressed to him, and the arrival of Mr. Nicolayson, have induced me to waive 
every other consideration, and to prepare for a strenuous endeavour to overcome 
the Porte’s objections to a completion of the sacred edifice. The grounds upon 
which our demand is now to be placed appear, in some degree, to open a better 
prospect of success. The proposed connexion of the church with the dwelling- 

ouses assigned to the British and Prussian Consuls, ought to operate powerfully 
in favour of a satisfactory arrangement; but I question whether it would be 
sate to bring forward the precedent of the former Jacobite church, which was 
subsequently converted into a mosque, and which did not stand upon the exact 
apot where the foundations of the new Protestant chureli are laid, 

I lament to say, that in sounding Rifaat Pasha to-day upon the subject, | 
met with no encouragement; and although, in obedience to your Lordship's 
instructions, I fully intend to obtain a more complete hearing, and to avail 
myself of any assistance which M, Le Coq may be disposed to lend for 
that purpose, it is my firm persuasion that the seruples of the Porte, whatever 
they may be, will not give way to our joint solicitations. 

| have, &c. 


(Signed) STRATFORD CANNING.

No. 24. 


Sir Stratford Canning to the Earl of Aberdeen —(Reecived May 22.) 


(No. 85.) 


My Lord, Constantinople, May 3, 1844, 

On further communication with Rifaat Pasha respecting the Protestant 
church at Jerusalem, his Excellency has informed me definitively that the 
Council is not prepared to reconsider its former unfavourable decision, but that 
enquiries have been addressed to the Pasha of Seyda, with rererence to the 
buildings proposed to be erected at Jerusalem for the eventual accommodation 
ofthe British and Prussian Consuls, and that in the event of Assand Pasha‘s 
returning a satisfactory answer, no objection will he made to a resumption of 
the interrupted works, it being understood that whatever part of the buildings 
may hereafter be used as a consular chapel, it will be so cireumstanced as j\o1 
to attract obtrusively the public attention. 

I venture to hope that the Pasha of Seyda, under the influence and jiuli- 
cious representitions of Colonel Rose, may be induced to address a favouralic 
report to the Grand Vizier, and in that case that all essential difficulties will 
in due time be practically removed, and the huildings. which are partly planned, 
he finished in ‘such a manner as to comprise the chureh of which the foundations 
are already laid, 


Lam happy to add, that agrecably to my request, orders will be given to 


secure Her Majesty's Consul at Jerusalem in the possession of the small plot of 


wround which he purchased several years ago, under the sanction of the Eeyp- 
tian Government, as a place of burial for British subjects, and to empower him 
to surround it with walls. 

My application to this effect originated in the complaints addressed ta 
Colonel Itosée and myself by Mr. Young, against the interference of Hayder 
Pasha of Jerusalem, and the attempt which that functionary had made to 
unsettle the Consul’s right to the ground, 

Lhave, &e. 
(Signed) STRATFORD CANNING. 


No. 25. 


Consul Wood to the Karl of Aberdeen.—(Recetved May 19.) 
(No, 9.) 
My Lord, Damascus, March 23, 1844, 
I HAVE the honour of submitting respectfully to your Lordship’s perusal 
copy of my report No 5 (Inclosure under No. 1) to Her Majesty's Ambassador, 
respecting the secession of about one hundred and fifty Greeks from their faith 
in behalf of the English chureh, | 

Although it is evident that no British subjects were connected with the 
tbove incidents notwithstanding the general impression, the seceders have 
judged it necessary, nevertheless, to address two memorials to this Consulate 
(Inclosures Nos. 2 and 3), with the hope of procuring its support and protection 
in case the Turkish authorities interfered with them, 

As such an event could not fail to rouse the suspicion and jealousy of the 
locnl, foreign, and native ecclesiastical authorities, | deemed it most prudent tu 
avoul tinking any reply to these memorials, which could only have added to the 
ombarrassment of my position at Damascus, already weakened by it. Inde- 
pendently of this consideration, it was manifest from the tone assumed by the 
Vasha's secret council, that it was prepared to forward to Constantinople, sueh 
representation of this peculiar transaction in reference to our supposed inter- 
ference, as might have impeded, in some degree, the successful issue of the 
negotiation now pending there, by exciting the doubts and fears of the Porte, 
aud of the other European Governments, whose respective agents here were 
ready to transmit to them the exaggerated, though in appearance plausible, 
accounts they had received of it,

The matter appeared, however, of suificien) import his Secret Council 
to authorize its being made the subject of warm discussion m his ’ 


17 


composed of his Excellency and Kahyia, of Kara Ali Pasha, the Emir-el-Hadj, 
the Soura Eminy, the Defterdar and Suleiman Effendi—this latter of whom 
argued that it was singular, at the very moment the British Government were 
earnestly asking of the Porte religious toleration in behalf of her Rayahs, that 
British subjects should be found in these parts converting them to the British 
Church, and endeavoured to explain, on this assumption, the secret motives 
which had instigated Her Majesty’s Government to it; wpon which Karably 
Pasha, following the dictates of a fanatical disposition, proposed to have recourse 
to measures of force to compel the Greeks, who had abjured their faith, to re- 
turn to it, which his Excellency overruled, and decided instead to address Assud 
Pasha on the subject, and to seek explanations from him with respect to the 
supposed connexion or interference of the foreign agents or their subjects in the 
secession of the Rayahs of the Porte from a church reeosnized by her. 

In the aaa the seceders, apprehensive of the consequences to them- 
selves of the step they had taken at Bevrout, addressed me a memorial (a 
translation of which I have the honour of inclosing herewith), which having re- 
mained without any acknowledgment whatever from me, was followed hy 
anger which also, from various cogent considerations, remained without a 
reply. 

The Greek Patriarch, on the other hand, dreading the effects of a division 
in his chureh, called on me to ask for explanations, which gave me the oppor- 
tunity of assuring his fminence that he may rest satisfied the servants of Her 
Majesty's Government in Syria had it for rule never to interfere in the religious 
affairs or dissensions of the people, and that he may, therefore, consider as a 
malicious misrepresentation anything said or advanced which identified them 
with transactions of the nature he complained of, 

It is, however, with deep regret [ venture to state, very respectfully, to 
your Excellency that all my efforts to appease the excitement which the seces- 
sion of 140 Greeks from their church has created against us have hitherto 
roved ineffectual, on account of the suspicion and jealousy to which this event 
las given rise in the breasts of the Turkish, foreign, and ecclesiastical authori- 
ties, and of the people at large, who believe, or feign to believe, that we are 
favourable to proselytism, inasmuch as it may facilitate hereafter the invasion of 
this country—an assertion which is studiously circulated by those who are 

anxious to witness the subversion of British influence in Syria, and which it is 
to be presumed will be repeated in the reports of the Turkish and foreign 
agents to their respective Governments. 

After maturely weighing the mischievous consequences to us both here and 
elsewhere, under present circumstances, of so groundless an imputation, I 
deemed it my duty to address a remonstrance to his Excellency Ali Pasha, 
disavowing in the most formal manner the supposed connexion of British sub- 
jects with the peculiar transaction of Hasheya, with the more immediate view, 
however, of rendering him more cautious and eireumspect in the manner, tone, 
and spirit he writes to the Porte respecting if by to-day’s conveyance. 

Tn conclusion, [ may be permitted respectfully to state that my attitude 
and position here have been considerably weakened by it, which I infer from 
the withdrawal of the confidence I hitherto enjoyed of the Turkish and ecelesi- 
astical Authorities of all denominations, who now seek the advice and counsel of 
other foreign agents.

(Signed) RICHARD WOOD.

besides such other allegations as appeared to me to implicate greatly the Prince 
himself. 

The bearer of Dr. Van Dyke's letter being himself one of the number of 
those who had been menaced and ill-treated, [ requested him to make a written 
deposition of the facts, which formed the subject of the complaint referred to 
me: and [ liave the honour of submitting to your Lordship’s perusal a transla- 
tion (Inclosure No. 1) in exeulpation of the Emir, who does not appear to 
deserve the imputations cast upon lim. 

The conviction however that, unless something was clfected to discourage 
the first indications of a persecution of the seceders by their own relatives and 
countrymen at the instigation, perhaps, of the Greck clergy, open violence and 
direet compulsion may finally follow, induced me to direet Michael Mushaka, 
the bosom friend of the Emir, to write to him, in his name, a confidential letter 
(Inclosure. No, 2) in a sense to impress him strongly with the notion that, his 
awn interest not less than that of the people entrusted to lis care, required that 
he should interfere eflicaciously to put a stop to every excess, which originated in 
religious dissensions, 

The Count de Portalis, Prussian Secretary of Legation at Constantinople, 
“ho happened to be at Hasheya at the time of its receipt, has since informed 
me that it had produced the desired effect; and that it had even so far intimi- 
dated the young Princes as to make them reiterate their invitation to the 
Rev. Mr. Smith to remain with them in order to sereen their father from ground- 
less calummnies and imputations. 

The Count has, moreover, informed me that, such is the intense interest 
which His Majesty the King of Prussia takes in the welfare of the Protestants 
in the East, that he has determined on proceeding to Constantinople to make 
his report on those of Hasheya to the Prussian Minister there previous to his 
return to Berlin, though he was of opinion that, as the whole matter or question 
was not sulliciently advanced, its management should be left to those on the 
spot until such time as its progress justified its being made the subject of formal 
communication with the Porte. 

I naturally gave him every assurance that, although was far from deeming 
myself authorized to grant British protection to a community of Protestants, 
subjects of the Porte, I would nevertheless exert all the means in my power to 
protect them from persecution and violence. 

Aali Pasha’s secret instructions to the mir, previous to his departure for his 
wlistrict, were that he should call on the Greek Patriarch and appease him; 
that he should keep the people quiet, and that he should do nothing to excite 
attention, which last part of his instruction confirms me in my belief that his 
Exeellency having replied to his Government's demand for explanations respect- 
ing this secession in the sense stated ii my report No, 17 of 20th May last to 
your Lordship, he is now doubly anxious to avert the recurrence of any incident 
that may contradict his statement, 

But were it otherwise, so long as the Kmir entertains the impression that 
the retention of his situation depended materially on the countenance and sup- 
port of Her Majesty’s Consulate, self interest will always lead him to abstain 
carefully from losing the benefit of the influence, which, when exerted, procured 
for him, for the second time, his appointment to Hasbeya. 

Lam, &e. 


(Siened) |" RICHARD WOOD.

Deposition of Nicola Haslab, 


(Translation.) June 27, D844. 


ON the day of the arrival of Emir Saad-el-Deen at Hasbeya, ‘Tanus-el-

No. 28, 


Consul Wood ta the Earl of Aberdeen.—(Received September 11.) 
(No. 23.) 


My Lord, Damascus, August 3, 1844, 

[IN continuation of my despatch, No..21, of the Sth of July, relating to 
the Greeks, who had seceded from their own Church, and had united themselves 
to the Church of England, I have the honour to state that the incessant 
intrigues of the Greek clergy and others having brought about a state of 
things in Hasbeya dangerous to every individual member of the Protestant 
community, whose lives were publicly menaced, sentiments of humanity no 
less than the personal interest I naturally take in their happiness and well- 
heing, induced me, on the receipt of the Rey. Mr. Smith's letters, (of which | 
have the honour to inclose extracts under Nos, 1 and 2,) conveying to me the 
peril of their position, to seck a private interview with the Pasha, which resulted 
in the transmission of a peremptory order (Inclosure No, 3,) to the Emir Saad- 
el-Deen, to prevent further violence from being offered to them by the Greeks 
of Hasheya, and those of dahleh, Rasheya, and Keefeir, who had repaired to 
the former place, with the intent of forcing or persuading them to return to 
their old creed, and in the express deputation of an officer to examine inta 
the whole matter, 

I have, likewise, addressed the Emir on the subject (Inclosure No. 4,) 
whose replies (Nos. 5 and G,) I venture respectfully to submit the more readily 
to your Lordship, as they are not only corroborated by Mr. Alison's opinion, 
formed on the spot, that the persecution and violence complained of were not 
of the alarming nature they were deemed in the first instance, but aga further 
evidence of the strict injunctions of the authorities to prevent their recurrence 
in future. . 

_ But in order to give more effect to the order of the Pasha, which extreme 
bigotry might have set aside, I have waited on the Greek Patriarch, and, after 
some observations from me, he was persuaded to address a patriarchal letter 
(No.7) to the Greeks and their clergy, to abstain heneeforward from molesting, 
in any manner whatsoever, the converts, with whom they were to live in peace 
and harmony according to the spirit and precepts of their Charch. 

The object of the above ordera was tu empower the Emir, on the one hand, 
to act more energetically, and to enjoin the Greeks, on the other, to conduet 
tllemselves peaceably and friendly towards their seceded countrymen, 

Your Lordship will be pleased to learn with satisfaction that both the 
Pasha and the Emir have given me every private and formal assurance, that 
they will exert their utmost effortsas far as circumstances and the embarrass- 
ment of their delicate positions will allow, to proteet these poor converts from 
violence; and the only incident, therefore, that gives me some cause for appre- 
hension is the direct and injudicions interference of other parties, in the hope 
af either bringing about their recantation, or of atfording them protection,

his present course. My hope is that you may be able to send him such official 
communication as shall embolden him to act, not in the way of positive protec- 
lion, but of dispensing equal justice and keeping the tewlics in check. Our 
friends here are waiting your answer. If it is not such as to relieve them, they 
will inove away. Inquiries have been already sent off fora place, But I need 
not say that this would be a great calamity, and attended with suffering as 
many of them are poor. 


(Signed) E. SMITH, 


Inclosure 2 in No. 28. 
The Rev. E. Smith to Consul Wood. 


(Extract,) Hasheya, July 1G, 1844, 

I WROTE you on the 12th by two messengers, who have not yet returned. 
Since then events have occurred which [ deem of importance to communicate, 

Yesterday some twenty or thirty horsemen arrived from Zahlch, Rasheya 
and Keefeir, sent by the Patriarch to restore the seceders to the Greek church. 
They were unarmed, and profess to have come only to use means of persuasion. 
All of them at first dismounted at the house of Shehin Gabrin, the head of the 
seceders, and then divided themselves among the houses of a few of the other 
leading men, but these, having previous notice of their coming, had absented 
themselves, fearing the inconvenience and expense of harbouring them for the 
several weeks which they threatened to stay, unless they accomplished their 
purpose sooner, 

In the evening our friends went in a body to the Emir, to ask him to 
remove from their houses these persons, who had thus quartered themselves 
upon them. The Emir yielded to their request; but on sending out his orders, 
found the young men’s party rising in arms to resist him and cut off the 
seceders, and the town was at once in great commotion, ‘The Emir immediately 
forbade our friends to leave the palace, and sent for me. On my arrival, he 
declared that the town was in rebellion, and having no means of quelling the 
riot, he had assembled his family, and had determined to throw down the reins 
of government and leave the next morning. He justified our friends {rom all 
blame, and was loud in reproaching their enemies, but what to do he confessed 
he knew not. It was a moment of no little anxiety, But at this juncture the 
Druse Sheiks, Amin Shems and Mahommed Keis appeared, and with a generosity 
and order I shall never forget, threw themselves into the breach, and declared 
that their own blood should be shed before a single Protestant should be touched 
hy their enemies, This seemed to give the Emir confidence ; his orders took 
cifect, and the town was soon quieted, I wish to call your attention particularly 
to this noble conduct of the Druse Sheiks; for I belive it was the union of 
their influence with that of the Emir which saved us, That this friendly dis 
position of theirs should be encouraged, is of great importance. Nothing could 
encourage it so much as a letter from yourself to them, commending their con- 


duct in thus supporting the Emir’s authority, and one to the Emir reecommend- 
ing him to restore them more fully ee confidence, Such a step would at 


ee 


—- 


| : 
once break down the young men’s party, which is now the great instrument of 
ersecution, Se i: 

pers Toiday the town has been tolerably quiet again, but the people coe 
Zahleh, &c,, are still here, and what they will finally do is not yet known. Ir. 
Thompson will keep you informed. He has arrived to-day and will take my 


| place, while I return to Beyrout. (Signed) E SMITH.

Inclosure 3 in No. 28. 
Visterial Letter to Emr Saad-el-Deen. 


(Translation.) Damascus, July 17, 1844. 


THE Rayahs, who have recently become members of the aan 
Chureh, have presented & memorial to us, complaining of the conduct of \ i 
‘Greeks of Hasbeya, who are persecuting and molesting them for having 
separated themselves from their creed; and that, 1m particular, a a, Y 

name Giorgios Ghorrah, has formed a party of his own sect, at the Be 
of which he has placed himself, and has given them authority to ill-treat @ 
such who have beeome Protestants. ‘They further state that they (the memo- 
rialists) are also the loyal subjects of the Porte, to which they pay the tribute 
) and taxes demanded of them, and request, therefore, that justice may be done 
| them, that they may be protected against violence, and that they may bs 
allowed to live in peace, in order to attend to their. several avocations an 
affairs. ‘Che foregoing forms the contents of their petition. a 
Hi We have, in consequence, issued this our order to you, anil of which 
Rustum Effendi is the special bearer, that you may examine into the violent 
: proceedings of the Greeks, at the instigation of the said Georgios Ghorrahy 
towards the seceders. In the event that the complaint be correct, you must 
' know that any violence or molestation from one set of Rayahs towards another, 
‘ie it on account of the reason stated above, or from any other motive what- 
soover, is contrary to the Imperial will and to our own wishes, and that it 15 
impossible for us to permit it, under any consideration whatever, 
You are called upon, therefore, to give me minute explanations thereon, 
and if it be true that one ltayah molests another, you must prevent it effec- 
) tively and efficaciously, and inform us of the name of the offender, the cause, 
l 


and in what manner the assault was made, that after an examination into it, 
we may issue the proper orders for the punishment of the culprit, | 
Althouwh those who may have changed their faith have deviated from the 
ath of their forefathers, yet this matter can only be taken cognizance of by 
the Sublime Porte, and it does not absolutely appertain to the Rayals to puns 
L each other, You are, therefore, bound to hinder and prevent them from such 
harsh proceedings, which are contrary to the Imperial will; for all are the 
cubjcets of the Sublime Porte, and you are, in consequence, obliged to deal 
with them all as heretofore. Inform us of these occu Sune in detail and with 
nes ay ve strict attention to these presents. : 
| exactness, aml pay ye stric tt it (L. 8) NARITA 


Inclosure 4 in No. 28. 
4 | Consul Wood to Emir Saad-el- Deen. 
it ; Damascus, July 17, 1844. 
Translation.) amascus, July 17, 1844 
1 HAVE to inform you that Nicola Haslab and Halil-el-Houri, of 
| the community of professing Christian Protestants of . Hasbeya, have come here 


to compluin ot the violence used Cet them by the Greeks. 
First, By taking away some © their arms ; | 
Beans, By curalOg and swearing at them, their harems, and daughters, 
when going to divine Service; 


— So OO 


aa 


Thirdly, By striking one of them, Halil-el-Houri, under your palace ; and 

Fourthly, By ill-treating and using improper language towards them and 
their ministers, and by deputing an individual to, kill one of the teachers. 

The chief abettors of these proceedings art Giorgios Ghorrah and the 
sons of Shehin Asseff, After Michael Mushaka “had written to you to prevent 
the ill-treatment of the Protestants, the Druses and Maronites ceased their 
persecution, and eonducted themselves with civility towards them, but Giorgios 
Glorrah assembled and headed an armed crowd, commanded by Boulouk 
Bashis (officers), and assumed the power of giving directions, and of doing 
whatever he pleased, . 

I was not only surprised to hear’ the foregoing statement, but was also 
astonished that you should have permitted such proceedings to have occurred 
under your very palace, which would lead one to suppose that you either 
connived at them, or were too weak to punish the daring offenders, who have 
thus disregarded the respect that is due to your ollicial character, Your 
weakness and their presumption may cause you vexation hereafter. 

You are already acquainted with the representations made to the Sublime 
Porte by the Great Powers, in behalf of religious toleration, and with the 
Imperial assurance to them that no one should be molested for his religion ; 
besides which sufficient explanations have been given by his Excellency the 
British Ambassador to Rifaat Pasha and the Greek Patriarch on the secession 
of the Greeks at Hasheya. 

I have likewise received to-day a letter from Colonel Rose respecting the 
‘IL-treatment of the seceders, which he has some difficulty in believing, seeing. 
that you are the Governor of the district wherein it has oecurred, unless, 
indeed, it be with your secret connivance; otherwise he must conclude that 
you are not in a position to administer to the people confided to your care, or 
cause justice to be dispensed equally to all, Colonel Rose has thus seen 
himself’ forced to represent this state of things to the Capudan Pasha, more 
especially as the Foreign Vowers are resolved on denouncing and preventing 
any occurrence, which tends to check religious toleration. 

[have consequently thought it necessary to address you on this subject, 
in order that you may prevent and hinder violence and persecution in matters 
of religion by the punishment of the offenders, in accordance with the duty 
which devolves on you to protect all Rayahs equally, of whatever denomination. 
But, in case you are unable to chastise Giorgios Ghorrah, or others of the 
culprits, inform me of it, that I may ask of the local Government their arrest, 
translation hither, and punishment. 

T shall have the honour of waiting on his Excellency Sali Pasha, to speak 
to him on this matter, that he may remove all such causes of molestation and 
violence as might lead to quarrels and strife. 


(Li. 8.) RICHARD WOOD.

Inclosure 4 in No, 28. 


Emir Saad-el-Deen to Consul Wood. 
(Tranalation.) 
(After Compliments.) Hasbeya, July 18, 1844. 

I HAVE had the honour to receive at a propitious hour the letters ot 
your Seignicurie, the one in reference to the representations made by Nicola 
Haslab and Halil-cl-Houri, and the other relating to the vistt of Mr, Alison, and 
[ have understood all that you were pleased to address to me. 

In regard to Mr. Alison, he has honoured our place, and we have had some 
conversation together, for Tam always anxious to do whatever may be agree- 
able to you, and to assist your Excellency (your Seigneurie) in every thing 
that you may desire, to the utmost of my power and means, particularly when 
my doing 50 may be pleasing to your magnificent Government, I need sy 
no more, for your own heart knows more of my sentiments than [can pees 

Respecting the individual who insulted the daughters of Nicola Haslah, | 
imprisoned him, but on examination, finding that the whole complaint was 
futile, I authorized his liberation, .48 to Halil-el-Houri, I never beard anything 
about his affair until the receipt of your letter. 


——— 


28 


Rest assured'that with the influence of my superiors, and with yorr good 
offices, I will content all parties, and leave no room for complaint from any of 
them; and should there ever be any the slightest difference in my bearing 
towards them, your wisdom and sagacity will indicate to you from what it 
might arise, All are the subjects of my Effendi, and it behoves me to dispense 
equal and impartial justice to each and every one of them, &c. &e., 

(L. 8.) EMIR 8AAD-EL-DEEN. 


Inclosure 6 in No, 28. 


Emir Saad-el-Deea to Consul Wood. 
(Translation.) 


(After Compliments.) July 19, 1844, 


THE letter you were pleased to address to us, reached us at a propitious 
moment, and we have perfectly understood all the explanations you have given 
us respecting the necessity of treating with equality the Protestants and Greeks 
of Hasheya, and of hindering’ the Greeks from ill-treating the Protestants, in 
accordance with the agreement of the great Powers with the Porte for the 
prevention of all violence and persecution in matters of religion, by which every 
one acauires the full liberty of following his conscience in regard to his faith, 

Under the same date, we had the honour of receiving a strong buyu- 


- ruldi from his Excellency Aali Pasha delivered to us by Mahommed Shekir 


Effendi on this subject, to prevent every violence and vexation to the Protest- 
ants ; and in obedience to his Excellency's orders and your statement and wishes, 
we published forthwith his buynuruldi to us, and ordered everybody to con- 
duct themselves properly and in conformity thereto, 
It is purely my duty toreflect seriously on matters of administration, to 
reserve equality among the people, and to protect them without making any 
distinetion between them. If it pleases the Almighty, therefore, we will hinder 
all causes that may give rise to Resieniiia between mdividuals of whatever 
nation they may be; and in case anything occurs to require punishment, we are 
either to represent it immediately to the Government, or to proceed to the 
chastisement of the offender, according to his deserts, but that in case it is of 
no importance, purely accidental, or arising from ignorance, we ure then to 
punish the culprit summarily, and as much as circumstances will allow, without 
troubling his Excellency with it, Being an obedient servant of the Government, 
we are bound at all times to administer matters in a way to secure the tran- 
quillity of the people of these parts, without evincing any, the slightest, parti- 
ality for any one, agreeably to the high pleasure of his Excellency and your 
wishes; for we know this to be your desire for our own sake, &c. &e. 
iL. 8.) EMIR SAAD-EL-DEEN. 


Inclosure 7 in No. 28. 
The Patriarch of Damascus to the Greeks of Hasbeya. 


(Translation.) spamascus, July 20, 1844, 
WE have been informed that certain persons among you have interfered, 
and have used acrimonious language towards some of those who have embraced 
the Protestant religion, more particularly on the arrival of some of my flock 
from Zahleh, who eame for the purpose of restoring peace and harmony among 
you, according to your local usages, and in order to remove all such feelings 
as may arise from interested motives, and to restore love and purity into the 
hearts of the people. Woe were excessively grieved, however, on learning those 
things; which proceeded from the insulting and virulent language which has 
been used, as foreign to the spirit of the Church and to the preaching of charity; 
because our ancient orthodox and holy Church, founded upon the basis of the 
Apostles and Prophets, has never had, from the days of the holy Apostles up to this 
perio, recourse cither to force or to the sword for gaining people unto the faith. 
It is trae that the Chureh, strong in itself in simpleness of heart and 
purity of intention, acts in this spirit towards all other ereeds and nations who 


oe 


= tt 7 iam 


29 


return to it. But in the same manner as the holy Apostles drew men to the 
religion of Christ by the purity of evangelical teaching, and by their good and 
virtuous deeds and moral propriety, which is like unto the transcendent splen- 
dour of the light, so also did our fathers act, and let us also conduct ourselves 
with the same decorum. If it be, therefore, your object to bring over your 
brethren to their original faith and to the bosom of their benevolent mother, 
you must behave towards them with kindness and love, so that by their return 
to you in love and the spirit of the Gospel, ye may gain both them and the 
approbation of God, and be likened unto the Saints, bearing fruit unto 
wood, 

~ Our demand therefore is, that ye communicate these words that we write uuto 
you to each other, and reflect upon them, and that ye obviate altogether all causes 
of scandal, not only among men, but also among the women and children, not only 
in the streets, but also in the houses whether the inmates be present or absent, 
In short, you are to treat them with brothers’ love, whichis agreeable both to the 
Lord and to us, for you are bound to act thus towards them, namely, not to 
give them any cause whatever for irritation, because you will thus be con- 
demned by your own conscience and by the Most Holy, and ye will also sulfer 
the blame of men, Every man is accountable to God for his own actions in 
the Day of Judgment, and he will receive the wages thereof, be they good or 
bad. If, therefore, every man is to be tried for his actions, and to be rewarded 
accordingly, it follows that no man will be accountable for the deeds of another, 
nor will he be punished for them; but every one is bound to labour in all 
things which are pleasing unto God, and he will himself derive the benefit 
thereof, both in this world and in the world to come, and to abstain from all 
things that concern him not, in order not to hear that which pleascth him not. 
We, therefore hope that ye will act according to our directions, and keep our 
injunctions that we may always pray unto the most High God to preserve and 
keép you, &e, 


Inclosure 8 in No. 28. 
M. Basily to Emir Saad-el-Deen. 


(Translation, ) 
(After the customary Compliments.) Beyroul, July 24, 1844, 


THE renown of your good name from old made us desirous for an oppor- 
tunity to address you, which having presented itself, we have great pleasure in 
congratulating you on your appointment, which you owe to the confidence the 
Government reposes in you on account of the services which your ancestors 
and yourselves have rendered for so long a period, and for having attached the 
people to you. 

Undoubtedly the inhabitants of Hasbeya and Rasheya must be perfectly 
contented and happy in possessing a Governor like yourself, particularly after 
their sufferings from the oppression of Bozo Agha, who destroyed their peace 
for a time, toa degree of obliging a portion of the Christians of those parts 
to abandon their chureh, deceived as they were by strangers, who are exerting 
their utmost efforts to establish a new religion in these countries, 

As, however, our illustrious master (the Emperor) protects the Church 
(Greek), it (its protection) appertains to us likewise; and in consequence, there- 
fore, of our friendship and amity with the Pashas of Saida and Damascus, we 
have acquainted them with the true state of the affair, which shortly led to the 
dismissal of Bozo Agha, Your nomination, therefore, to the government of 
those parts clearly shows that it is for the repose of the Christians there ; and 
as some of them have returned to their true church, our notion thereon has 
been so far confirmed, i 

The surprising and painful intelligence, however, that only a short time 
ago fresh intrigues have occurred at your place, and that several individuals 
who ought not and who cannot on any pretext whatsoever interfere in the 
affairs of the people, have written to you letters under the impression of

ond. Oh! Emir, whose religion is that of Aslamism, what is it to you if 
some of the Christians follow the Greek or the Protestant faith? 1 think that 
itis not worthy of your dignity, nor eoniueive to your interests, to make your- 
self an instrument in the hands of others in order that they may accomplish 
their. purposes through you, in an affair which belongs solely to religion an 


conscience. 
4rd and finally, we state to you, that what has occurred in Hasbeya has 


been represented by the Consuls-Jeneral of the two great Governments of 
Prussia and England to their Embassies at Constantinople, as well as hy the 
Jocal Government to the Sublime Porte; and that we are actually waiting for 
the answers from that quarter. It is now in consequence superiluous and out 
of time for those who are under direction of superior authorities to attempt 
uselessly the termination of this matter in a way contrary to the interests and 
wishes of two of the greatest allied Governments. } 

It now rests with you to judge whether it is expedient for you, with all the 
serious consequences that may accrue from it to yourself, to interfere in a 
matter of religiou which does not appertain to you in any manner whatsoever. 
But it will be construed unto an unfavourable meddling on your part, so long 
as you permit that ealumny and violence should be exercised towards those who, 
[ tinderstand, have not swerved from their duty of loyal subjects, without your 
punishing the offenders. If you allow them to be molested, you will fail in the 
execution of the duties of your office. Believe me, therefore, that the best ad- 
vice I can give youis, to exert your utmost efforts for the prevention of violence 
towards them, and to leave it (the affair), in the hands of those superior au- 
thorities to whom its definition belongs. 

Know you also, that this matter neither appertaing to you, to the Pasha of 
Damascus,.or to the Greek Patriarch, but solely to the Sultan, Do your utmost 
to keep matters in the best possible position, and prevent those scenes which 
have lately oceurred in Hasbeya, and punish the culprits. If you are in 
possession of instructions from, the Sublime Porte to ill-treat those who embrace 
the Protestant faith, we have nothing more to add: but if, as we positively 
know, you have not received any special instructions about it, it is better for 
you not to act before you do get them, 

Do not allow yourself to be induced by others to follow their wishes, that 
you may not repent of it shortly, for repentance then will avail you naught, 
Believe my word, O you Prince! that thore are authorities whose hands are 
longer and their positions much more elevated than those of Aali Pasha or the 
Patriarch. You had better, therefore, wait the issue of this affair, and the de- 

termination of the superior authorities. Believe likewise, that the Protestants 
of Hasbeya, so long as they walk m the straight path, that is, do what is 
their duty towards their tovernment, will not be in need of friends and 
wellwishers to protect them and prevent their being oppressed. 

We have deemed it expedient to write this present letter, and to seni it by 
Mr. J. Catafazo, chief dragoman of the Prussian Consulate-General, that he 
may himself see with his eyes the posture of attairs, 

(Seal of Office) (Signed) LOUIS DE WILDENBRUCK, 
Consul-General of the Prussian Government 
for all Syria and Palestine.

No, 29. 


Congul-General Rose to Sir Stratford Canning.—(Received in London, Sept. 11.) 


(No. 37.) 
Sir, | Beyrout, July 26, 1844. 

_ IHAYVE the honour to state to your Excellency, that on the 14th instant, 
the Rev. Mr. Whiting, an American Missionary, came to me in some agitation, 
and informed me that the Rey, Mr, Smith, who is now at Hasbeya, in charge 
of the seceders from the Greek Antiochian Church, had requested him to call 
bee see delay, and represent to me the critical position of himself and 

e seceders, 


32 
Mr. Whiting then handed to me an extract of a letter from Mr. Smith, a 


copy. of which I have the honour to inclose to your Excellency. 

L said to Mr. Whiting, that a case of oppression of Christians at Hasbeya 
had been clearly proved; that, in strict conformity with my previous conduct, 
I should now act with due energy in protection of Christianity and the nghts 
of humanity, and that I should instruct Her Majesty's Consul at Damascus in 
that sense, to make the necessary representations to the Emir Saad-el-Deen, 
Governor of Hasbeya, 

Thad heard that the Emir had received a bribe from the Greek Patriarch 
at Damaseus, to act against the seceders. 

I, without loss of time, inclosed to Mr. Consul Wood the extract of the 
letter from Mr. Smith, and wrote to him as follows. 7 

“T beg to inclose to you © paper civen to me by Mr. Whiting, who isin 
much distress at the state of the converts in Hasbeya, It is clear that the 
Emir Saad-el-Deen is either acting under secret instructions, or has been 
bribed: but whatever be his motives we, as British functionaries, have a right 
to call upon him to treat Syrians under his care and government with humanity 
and justice. Our early instructions justify us in doing this; and the promises 
made to Her Majesty's Ambassador by the Sultan this year, as stated in His 
Excellency Sir Stratford Canning’s circular, justify us on calling on the Emir, 
Saad-el-Deen, as an authority of the Porte, to fulfil his Sovereign's engage- 
ments as stated in that circular. 

“Tn the sense of these observations and knowing from the kindness 
and humanity which you already displayed in the affair of Hasheya, that you 
will do what. is best, kee you to make a representation to the Emir Saad-el- 
Deen directly, or to the Pasha, on this subject. 

« You will see that a positive case of insult to a Christian, and oppression 
of a Christian are stated by a most respectable person. I think that the Emir 
Sand-el-Deen should be held responsible for sanctioning such outrages; it 1s 
clear that if he permits them, he should no longer hold an office of which he 
neglects most material and important functions, protection of peaceable sub- 
jects of the Porte from insult and outrage. 

« You are quite at liberty to make use of my name, and it would be better 
that you should do 50; and pray inform these functionaries, that if I hear 
another case of persecution or outrage sanctioned by the Emir Saad-el-Deen, 
1 shall make a special report of the Emir Saad-el-Deen to Her Majesty's 
Ambassador, and complain, moreoyer, to the Ca midan Pasha emphatically of 
the Emir Saad-el-Deen’s disobedience of the aan of his Sovereign, namely, 
that he wishes all classes of his subjects to be treated with Justice and 
humanity.” 

Your Excellency will perceive by the tenor of this, and my previous 
despatches relative to matters at Hasbeya, that whilst I have thus fully carried 
out the intentions of the Earl of Aberdeen that there should be no interference 
with the faith of the Christian sects in Syria, I have also given full effect to the 
humane instructions of Her Majesty's Government, and of your Excellency in 
favour of humanity and justice. 

Promise of protection, in the event of conversion, 18 very different from 
defence of converts against persecution, It only now rests with the seceders 
at Hasbeya to render their conversion a fait accompli, 

Our’ position, therefore, as regards the seceders at Hasbeya is perfectly 
good and defensible —that is, entire non-interference in favour of conversion, 
and, secondly, defenee of the seceders, on the same principle of justice and 
humanity which has been exercised in favour of all other Syrians, without 
regard to their ereed or persuasion. 

Indeed, the most bigotted would find it difficult to blame a Protestant 
oflicer for exercising those good offices in favour of Protestant Syrians, which 
he has often and so effectually brought into action for the benefit of all the 
other sects in Syria,—Christians of all sects, Mussulmans, Metualis, Jews, 
and others. 

It is impossible to be blind to the political considerations which result from 
the conversion of Hasbeya. They are chequered with good and evil; the 
former, I think, predominates, if discretion takes the place of over-geal and 

enthusiasm. 


33 
Mr. Consul Wood having asked my opinion, I thought it fai 
: ht it fair towards him 
to express my entire approval of the steps he had taken in protecti 
Hasbeya converts against oppression. eS sin! pro pfocsion of His 


_ Lhave, &e. 
(Signed) HUGH ROSE. 


Inclosure in No, 20. 


The Rev. E. Smith to the Rev. Mr. Thomson, 


(Extract) | Hasheye, July 11, 1844, 
WHEN I wrote by Butrous, T expressed the apprehension that the result 
of this week would be of great moment. Our cirennistances are now more 
critical than ever, and Lam ata loss to know what to do, | 
I had « long interview with the Emir on Monday, and Abu Besharah has 
seen him once since, Noone could speak fairer, or promise better than he 
But in fact our friends have experienced no relief from his return, 1 heir 
enemies liave become bolder. The abuse heaped upon them is constant, and ; 
instances have occurred even in the palace. Threats of extermination are made 
openly, not excepting myself, Yesterday, indeed, a man who had abused the 
daueliters of two or three of our most respectable men was imprisoned, but for 
not more than an hour, and in a way to make no impression, To-day one of 
our most respectable men was beaten near the entrance of the palace and when 
he male complaint, though actually wounded, the culprit was nob even blamed. 
The condition to whieh wey are reduced is insufferable. Indeed Lam in dail 
expectation that blood will be shed, and I have addressed a note to that effect to 
the Emir; but he does nothing except give me polite words and fair promises 
The stability of our friends in these circumstances is wonderful, As nearly 
as I can ascertain, the whole number that has left is not over twent ¥, The zeal 
of many, especially the women, 1s daily increasing. But they will not remain in 
Hasbeya in their present state of degradation and danger, Nor do I believe 
they can remain without an official communication from some one of the Pro- 
testant Consuls to the Emir. ‘This will give him a safe basis to act upon, if he 
is waiting for that; or will make him feel obliged to act, if he ts reluctant. 
Now what I beg of you is, that immediately on receiving this you will go 
to Beyrout, and seek an interview with the English and Prussian Consuls- 
General, My hope is that they may have received instructions to act. At any 
rate, you will get their advice. Pray be as expeditious as possible, for every 
hour, in our present circumstances is of great moment,

No. 32. 


Despatch to Baron Meyendorff, Russian Minister at Berlin, —{ Communicated to 
Viscount Palmerston by Baron Brunnow, September.) 


St. Pétersbourg, le }} Aodt, 1844, 
LA situation intérieure de la Syrie n’a cessé d’occuper la sollicitude de notre 


-auguste Maitre. Crest avec reeret que Sa Majesté a remarqueé les facheuses 


dissidences qui se sont manifestées sur cette question entre les représentans des 
Cing Grandes Cours 4 Constantinople; et ce qui a surtout peiné l'Empereur ce 
sont les machinations auxquelles les missionnaires se livrent depuis quelque 
temps en Syrie dans un esprit de prosélytisme, quine peut qu’ajouter un nouvel 
dément de trouble et de discorde a tous ceux dont empire Ottoman subit deja 
linilnence. 

Lorsque les missionnaires vont précher l'évangile aux peuples chez lesquels 
la parole du Seigneur n'a point encore pénetré, certes aucun lime, aucun re- 
proche ne s’attache a leurs pas, car ils ne font quwaccomplir une sainte vocation. 
Ils méritent, au contraire, de justes encouragemens. Mais il en est bien autre- 
ment, M. le Baron, lorsque ces efforts tendent & porter le schisme au tmilien des 
serviteurs du Christ, et 4 ébranler leurs antiques croyances. ‘Tout veritable 
Chrétien répronvera hautement de semblables essais, et ces tentatives de con- 
version assurheront, a ses yeux, un caractére doublement repréhensible lors- 
quelles n’épargnent pas méme le bercean du Christianisme, of tous les cultes 
devraient se reneontrer en paix et se donner la main pour se montrer dignes 
de leur foi et de leur origine communes. 

Telles sont les réflexions qu’a suggérées A l'Empereur Ja lecture des derniers 
rapports qui nous sont paryenus de la part de notre Consul-Général 4 Beyrout, Sa 
Majesté me charge d’en communiquer ci-prés un extrait a votre Excellence. Ilen 
résulte que les agens Prussiens semblent méconnaitre Vattitude dans laquelle ils 
auraient intéret Ase circonserire invariablement, et, qu'entraincs peut-ctre par un 
faux zele, ils accordent aide et protection Ades menées dont ils devratent repousser 
la responsabilité. Notre auguste maitre croit de son devoir d'appeler attention 
la plus sérieuse de la Cour de Berlin sur les inconveniens de faire poursuivre une 
pareille ligne de conduite 4 ses agens en Syne. Nous ne pouvons pas admettre 

welle soit conforme i leurs instructions, encore moins vin yolonté du Roi, et 
il suflira probablement de leur recommander plus de circonspection et d’impar- 
tialité pour faire rentrer leur action dans les limites dont elle n’aurait jamais di 
sortir. Lordre de 'Empereur, je vous engage, M. le Baron, & exprimer ce 
voeu au cabinet de Prusse. I nous est dicté dla fois par des considérations 
religieuses, et par des considérations politiques. Dun ecété, l'Empereur ne 
saurait oublier ce qu'il doit a ses co-religionnaires, et il s'opposera de la 
maniére la plus positive A tout ce qui pourrait tourner a leur ttriment; de 
autre coté, il a constamment present ason esprit, le maintien de Vordre et de la 
sécurité on Turquic, but principal des efforts de toutes les Puissances, Si done 
un prosélytisme inopportun est contraire aux convictions personnelles de Sa 
Majesté, si ce prosélytisme offre en Syrie, plus que partout ailleurs, des dangers 
réels, cea mémes convictions ne tarderont pas, nous aimons a l'espérer, d se 
communiquer également aux autres Puissances, qui dés-lors se joindront & 
nous pour étouffer dans leur germe des conflits religioux, en presence desquels 
il nous serait difficile 4 la shpee de rester simples spectateurs. Nous le 
répétons, M. le Baron, nulle part union et concorde ne semblent plus nécessaires 
que sur ce point, 

J'invite votre Excellence & porter la présente dépéche et son annexe a la 
connaissance du Ministére Prussien. 

Recevez, ke.

ge je Vai informé que je prétais dans cette occasion toute mon assistance au

voudrait pas se trouver en opposi

intimes, a écrit dans cette occasion unc lettre & Emir Saad-el-Deen, dans le but 
de l'intimider par les noms de ln Prusse et de l'Angleterre. Le Consul des

leurs efforts.

No, 3d, 
The Earl of Aberdeen to Sir Stratford Canning. 
(No. 161.) 
Sir, Foreign Office, September 20, 1844. 


1 HAVE to state to your Excellency that Colonel Rose transmitted to me a 
copy of his despatch to your Excellency, No. 37, of the 25th of July, 
respecting the Protestant converts in the Hasheya ; and [ now inclose, for your 
Excellency’s information, a copy of a despatch which I have in consequence 
addressed to him. 

I have addressed a despatch in the same terms to Her Majesty's Consul at 
Damascus. 

Your Excellency will perceive from the inclosed copy of a despatch from 
the Russian Government to their Minister at Berlin, which has been commu- 
nicated to me by Baron Brunnow, the feelings with which the Imperial Cabinet 
regard the attempts of the Protestant Missionaries in Syria to make proselytes 
from the Greek Churelh.
