No. 54, 


Consul-General Rose to the Earl of Aberdeen—(Received November 5.) 
(No, 42.) 


My Lord, Beyrout, October 7, 1844. 

I HAVE the honour to inclose to your Lordship copy of despatch to Bir 
Stratford Canning. 

I have the honour to say, that it was M. Basily who commenced the con- 
versation alluded to in his letter to me respecting the conversions at Hasbeya, 
on which occasion I diselaimed, as 1 had done before to other parties, all con-~ 
nexion with the American Missionaries, or attempts at conversion. 


37 


I did not fail to represent to the Prussian Consul-General the great regret 
which I felt at his having made.any mention of Great Britain or myself, as a 
servant of Her Majesty’s Government, in connexion with the conversions at 
Hasbeya, as he knew perfectly well that. I would have nothing whatever to do 
with the question of conversion, 

M. de Wildenbruch at first alleged in excuse something said by his Excel- 
loney Sir Stratford Canning, which I proved to be quite without foundation, and 
then palliated his error by a frank confession that he had done wrong, and by 
authorizing me to make the statement contained in my letter to the Russian 
Consul-General, which I told him (M. de Wildenbruch) was indispensable for 
the vindication of the consistency of my Government and myself, 

M. de Wildenbruch afterwards sent me a document, which, he states, was 
his original rough copy of his letter to the Emir Saad-el-Deen, which he gave 
for translation to his interpreter. 

M. Basily, on the other hand, persists that the copy which he sent me wie 
the real letter sent by M, dé Wildenbruch. 

Mr. Wood also sent me afterwards an extract of that letter which he hac 
seen, which eurresponds, as far as it goes, with the translation of the letter sen! 
me by Mr, Basily. However, I could only accept M, de Wildenbruch’s: acknow- 
ledgment of his own letter, | 

M. Basily informs me that the Treaty of Kainardji between Russia andl 
the Sublime Porte, gives to the former Power the right of protection of the 
Greek Chureh in the Sultan’s dominions. 

Your Lordship will see that I avoid any recognition of that right by SAYIN 
in my letter ;— 

Firstly.—* Quant au premier point, je ne Vaborde Re parcequ'll regaride 
une matidve du ressort des autorités supéricures spirituelles et temporelles des 
sujets Ottomans susdits." 

Secondly—** Au reste les assurances, ou declarations, que j'ai eu le plaisir 
de vous faire, cher collécue, je les ai faites 4 d'autres. .A vous, elles prenaient la 
forme dune simple information amicale.” 

t rather think that M. Basily regrets his having declared the protection of 
the Greek Church in ‘Turkey by Russia, He has since told me that his instruc- 
tions are to do what he can for it (the Church) till the matter becomes serious, 
but then not to press it. That is the meaning of the rather confused statement 
which he made. : 

M. Basily verbally now states to me, that “the visit ". of the Greeks of 
Zalieh and Rasheya to the dissenters in order to re-convert them was the act of 
the Patriarch, and that he did not approve it, But your Lordship will see that, 
in his letter, Mr. Basily defends the visit, 

M. Basily also reasoned in a manner which was extraordinary on the part 
of a servant of a legitimate Government,—that there was nothing culpable in 
the insurrectionary and oppressive conduct of the disorderly at Hasheya, because 
such occurrences were frequent in Turkey; in short, he advocated insurrection, 
Beas and anarchy, because, in this instance, they favoured the objects 
whieh he had in view, that is, the intimidation and re-conversion of the 
dissidents. 

M. Basily, after the receipt of my letter, showed much soreness, and sail 
to me with temper, “Oh, of course, your Government, after reading such a 
letter as yours to me can do nothing but approve the course which you and Mr. 
Wood have taken, and I can only pity my poor Church.” 

I replied that Mr. Wood and mayeelt had the sincerest respect for his 
Church, and that nothing, as I before stated, could be farther from our wish 
tlian to interfere with it; but, | added, that my letter was a simple detail of 
facts, of which I had offered to furnish to M. Basily the proofs, and that till 
those facts were disproved, they stood true, and Mr. Wood and myself justified, 
That we had merely interceded in favour of the oppressed, the rights of order, 
and the Sultan's authority, which had been essentially attacked by the dis- 
orderly of Hasbeya. That we had not advocated the cause of the dissidents as 
Protestants, but oppressed Syrians, 

_ MM. Basily said that in my letter 1 had gone into matters nob touched on 
his letter, in order to place Mr, Wood and myself in the right, and himself in 
the wrong, 

The fact is that M. Basily’s letter to me was insidious; and if f had not

Russian authority, as a superior and a protector. It is true that M. Basily 
watches over the preservation of order, 


Itis the Russian Consul-General’s aim to strengthen by every possible 
nes this religious sympathy, as dangerous to Turkey as it is profitable to 
tussin. 

But for this very reason, whilst M. Basily 
the religious tie between Russia and Turkish 
his efforts from the Turkish authorities, 

Thus M. Basily told me the other day, that his Government were most 
anxious not to cause any jealousy to the Turkish Government by a show of 
sympathy for Turkish Greeks, and that in this sense, when he sent assistance in 
money to Palestine to these parties, “he did so with the utmost secrecy,” 

The Greek Church in Palestine are full of costly presents from the Em- 
peror of Russia, When, then, the Greek prays, his thoughts must involuntarily 
turn at times towards the munificent and powerful benefactor of his church, 

But M. Basily’s zeal to increase the political influence of Russia leads him 
to overstep at times the bounds of caution; and probably the Turks, and cer- 
tainly his colleagues, are fully alive to his never-ceasing endeavours to increase 
the polititical influence of his Government by the powerful means of sympathy 
which the Greek subjects of Mussulman and debilitated Turkey must feel for so 
puissant and so close a neighbour as Russia, whose religion is the same, and 
whose sympathy is so constant and so warm. 

It was then with despair that M. Basily saw the defection at Hasbeya, 
which threatened to be contagious; and in the conversation to which he alludes 
in the first part of his letter, he told me that he should bee the Pasha to drive 
out the American Missionaries from Hasbeya. I advised M. Basily not to do 
anything violent, which would bring him into collision with the Ameriean autho- 
rities or public opinion, and he desisted, 

Allowance must be made for M, Basily, as all feel a 
their own faith; but this encouragement and defence of anarchy and oppression 
cannot be exeused, Moreover, by his conduct he has hurt his own cause, 

In my despatch No. 37, 1 had the honour to state the position which, in 
the spirit of the instructions of the Earl of Aberdeen, I thought Her Majesty's 
servants in Syria ought to assume relatively to the converts at Hasbeya, and in 
the letter your Excellency will be pleased to perceive, I trust, that I have 
strictly retained a position which experience had proved, I venture to think, to 
be tenable and defensible. 

Your Exeelloney will observe that I do not acknowle 
M. Basily olltcially to protect the Greek Chureh in Syria. 

T have, &e. 
(Signed) HUGH ROSE.

No. 35. 
Consul Wood to the Earl of Aberdeen.——{ Received November 5.) 
(No. 30.) 
My Lord, Damascus, Seplember 11, 1544. 


WITH reference to my report, No. 23, of the 3rd of August, relative to the 
Hasbeyan conversion, I dare venture very respectfully to subjoin an extract of 
the Rev. Mr. Thomson's letter to me, inclosure under No. 1, as a further 
evidence of the violence and persecution to which the converts were unhappily 
subjected ; and which keine them finally to abandon their homes and to 
wander in the adjoining distriets, 

Previous to the accomplishment and execution of the plans of the 
Hasheyan Greeks against the seceders, I had endeavoured to counteract them 
by a sect of measures, which, as they emanated principally from the Turkish 
authorities themselves, ought to have been efficacious in their result, and would 
perhaps have been so but for the secret encouragement held out to the perse- 
cutors by other parties to persevere in a conduct that corresponded so well with 
their personal religious feclings, and with their desire to see every cause 
removed that called for the legitimate exercise of British influence. 

In addition to this, the unabating intrigues of the Greck clergy, the over- 
bearing conduct of the young men of Hasbeya, the arrogance of their own 
elected chiefs, who did not seruple to convey to me their intention of annihi- 
lating and destroying the seceders if they ever returned as Protestants, the 
prolonged absence-of the Pasha with all the disposable troops in the Hauran, 
and the more than ordinary weakness of the Emir whose personal attendants 
have even joined ina religions question, the adverse party leaving me no uther 
means of staying the current of events against the converts than by an appeal 
to the fears of the Head of the Greek Chureh in Syma, 1 sought and obtained 
an interview with the Patriarch. 

To all his observations | replied that Her Majesty's Government having 
received an assurance from the Sultan that Christians should not be molested 
on account of their religion, | could not remain a passive spectator of the 
violence of the Hashevans towards the converts; that the Christians of all 
denominations having availed themselves largely of the influence of the British 
Consulate to ameliorate ther civil condition in this Pashalic, | witnessed now 
with great pain their ingratitude and the ill use they were making of the very 
freedom which they owed to it; that if they were desirous that that influence 
should still be exercised to their advantage, they must give it better proofs than 
hitherto of their being worthy of it; that his Eminence must be aware that if 
this Consulate has hitherto abstained from pursuing severe measures agamet the 

persecutors, it was in consequence of the confidence it placed in him that he 
would exert his utmost efforts in putting # stop to the state of things which 
formed the subject of my complaint; but that if 1 was deecived in my hopes 
and the Hasbeyans dil carry inte exceution their threat of killing their seceded 
countrymen on their return, T should then deem myself justified in exerting 
myself to bring the assassins to an exemplary and an igneminijus punish- 
ment; and that in short, as the upholding of the principle of religious 
toleration in Syria had always formed one of the most gratifying tasks 
of Her Majesty's servants im this country, nothmg would atford them 
greater sitistaction than to see his Eminence enlist ina cause so worthy of his 
character and position, but that if he espoused the opposite side he may rest 
assured beforehand that however [might regret it, T should not shrink from the 
eontest, though hie must assume alone the whole of the responsibility of the 
consequences that might attend it, 
My firet language to the Patriarch was not altogether without some effect, 
for he addressed immediately a strong letter to the Hasbeyan Greeks, enjoining 
them to change their conduct and to use moderation, otherwise he would with- 
draw from their cause and leave them to their fate. 

But in order to make the Mussulman authorities take a more direct 
interest in this unfortunate religious dissension of Christians among themselves, 
with a view to its speedier termination, it became early important to change

by riding hard all night, reached Hasbeya about 9 o'clock in the morning, 
I found the place almost deserted. When I called upon the. Emir Saad-cl-Deen 
he gave me the following account, In the morning after T left, a Moslem from 
Beyrout, sent to collect debts, went into the market and'demanded payment of 
a small sum from one of the leadors of the young men. The man refused to 
pay, & quarrel arose; the Christian cursed the Moslem, his religion, Mahommed, 
the Sultan, &e., &c. The Moslem complained to the Emir, who called the man. 
and (as he told me), cursed him and his father and his religion, and ordered him 
to prison, The man said he would not go, fled out of the palace, gave the 
alarm, the shops were instantly shut, and ae mounted to the top of the hill 
where they encamped. ‘The first thing they did was to write an order to Sha- 
heen to leave the place hefore the “ Asser,” or they would kill him. At the 
same time they sent word round to the rest of the Protestants, that if they ac- 
companied Shaheen they would waylay them and murder them on the road. 
Shaheen sent off the messenger for me, and then went to the Emir, who told him 
he must go. He then went and shut up his establishments, whieh he had farmed 
of the Government, and delivered the keys to the Emir, and bid him farewell, 

The young men then drew up five demands, and sent them down to the 
Emir to accept and seal, as the conditions upon which they would return. ‘The 
first required the banishment of Shaheen, 'The second demanded the deposition 
of the Moslem cadi and the appointment of one of their party. The third re- 

uired that the Seeretary and Saraf should be of their party. The fourth that 
they should pay their debts and taxes (and without “ hawalies,” I believe). The 
lifth demand was, that if any thing happened requiring ‘the interference of the 
Emir, he should not doany thing before the individual was tried and committed 
before their cadi. About this fifth demand there is n difference of statement ; 
one is, that the Hmir should not punish at all, but that the young men would do 
it themselves. IT give it above as I understood it, ‘The Emir told me that he 
accepted and signed these demands, 

The Protestants, as soon as the others took arms, fled to the house of 
Shaheen, not knowing what to expect. When the order came to Shaheen to 
leave, they all resolved to stick together come what might, As the day 
advanced, and it became certain that all the Protestants would leave, the Emir 
and the sober part of the old men became alarmed, and sent word to Shaheen 
not to go until | got back; but ashe had been ordered to £0, first by their 
written command, and with the threat of death annexed, and, secondly, by 
the first demand as above, which had beon sealed by the Emir, and as the 
young men had not retracted their threat, and stond over hia head in arms, he 
reslved to go. 'The other Protestants having sent out spies to see that the roads 
were safe, also left the place, some by one road, some by another, and united 
together on the mountains towards Bumarich, The Emir blamed the Protest- 
ants for going, and said they had nothing to apprehend ; but they very naturally 
say, what security have we if we remain? the first. demand hanishes our chief 
man, Who alone can manage our affairs; the second puts 2 cadi° over us, who 
has become our enemy; and the fifth ties up the hands of the Emir from 
protecting us, even if he were disposed to do so, ‘The facts of this statement I 
had from the Emir himself before many witnesses, and as it agrees with what 1 
heard from our own people, I suppose it to be substantially correct. 

I told the Emir, that 1 had not ealled to lodge any complaints, but to bid 
him farewell, ‘That Thad nothing to say on the part of the Protestants, not 
having seen them. I called him and all persons present to bear witness that 
whatever consequences might result from this business, we had no share in the 
, Tesponsibilities incurred ; the whole affair had takon place in my absence, with- 

out my knowledge or advice. ‘To which they all assented, [then told them 
that we had come to Hasheya to be the eae teachers of this people, at 
their urgent and persevering request, and at sreat inconvenience to ourselves. 
Now the people had been driven out, we should go with them; if they returned 
and requosted us to come with them, we should do so. They all assured me we 
should be most welcome! Indeed the Emir appeared to be yery uneasy, if 
not alarmed, at the aspect of affairs, 

[packed up and started at 11 o'clock to join the company of Shaheen, 
which I reached nt daybreak, on a mountain above the Litany, on the 
road towards Muktara. They were nearly dead with eold and hunger, I had 
brought some bread with me, which they devoured like locusts, We reached

Muktara after dark, and were nobly entertained by Sheik Said. He entered 


warmly into the cause of these poor peop! ;

Consul Wood to the Earl of Aberdeen.—( Received November 5.) 
(No. 31.) 


My Lord, ae Damascus, October 9, 1844, 

. JN continuation of my report No. 80 of the Lith of September, I have 
the honour to submit very respectfully to your Lordship's perusal, two letters 
(Inelosures Nos, 1 and 2), lately addressed by the Cagninn Pasha and the 
Russian Consul-General of Beyrout to Aali Pasha, and having reference to the 
Hasbeyan question, 

The undiseuised suggestion of the former to the Pash 
that, instead of punishing those who were using their utmost efforts to effoot 
the recantation of the converts, they deserved hig countenance, that they 
may better accomplish their purpose, and the menaces of the latter supported 
by the unreserved declaration tht he would protest against every pro- 
ceeding which tended to the humiliation of the Hasbeyan Greeks, and to the 
consequent encouragement of the professing Christian Protestants coupled 
with the subile intrigues of the Patriarch, leaving me no longer any room to 
doubt that, unless some very efficacious steps were immediately taken, further 
complications would ensue, which would retard, and even render altovether 
doubtful, the return of the converts to (heir homes, I determined upon coming 
to an immediate understanding with his Exeellency on the subject, and for which 
purpose T waited upon him on the =vth ultimo, 

_ After reeapitulating to his Excellency all the circumstances connected with 
their expulsion, and with the unjustifiable and even rebellious roceedings of 
their rivals, I obtained his permission to convey to him, frank ¥ and without 
reserve, the observations I had to make, I then stated that, as Governor- 
General of this Pashalic, his Exeelleney either did or did not possess the 
means of protecting equally all the Rayahs of the Porte; that if he did possess 
them, out would not use them in behalf of the expelled converts, I must then 
consider him us their real though indirect persecutor; in which case, howevey 
muy regret it, still my duty would impose upon me the disa evable task of 
representing him as such to Her Majesty's Am assudor; and that if he leaded 
weakness as the cause of his lack of firmness in the matter, I should deem 
myself equally justified in ipprizing the same authority that the interests of the 
him to whom they 
presentative woulil 


& of Damascus, 


Sultan were sacrificed in this province by the feebleness of 
were entrusted. That in the former ease, Her Majesty's Re

not fail to look upon his conduct asa direct contravention of the assurance 
of the Sultan that Christians should not be persecuted on account of their 
religion in the Ottoman dominions; and in the latter it would remain with. his 
Government to consider whether it would permit its interests to be sacrificed 
any longer in his hands; but that in both cases the result could not but be 
injurious to him personally, ' | 

After a minute's silence, his Excellency replied, with Apparent candour, 
that, notwithstanding the erplexity of his position, caused by the letters of the 
Capudan Pasha and the Russian Consul-General as well as the representations 
of the Patriarch, he would, nevertheless, ayoid the imputation of having epboeed 
what might bé the wishes of Her Majesty’s Government; and requested to know 
what measures he should adopt that would be deemed satisfactory, 

T answered that, as we owed in a great measure the embarrassment of our 
relative positions to the weakness and want of energy of the Emir Saad-el-Deen, 
he should be recalled from the Government of Hasbeya, and his brother, Emir 
Halil, appointed in his room; that his Excellency should depute Sheik Mahommed 
Keiss, one of the feudal chiefs of that district, with a letter to the expelled con- 
verts inviting them to return forthwith, and whom he should accompany back to 
their homes and families immediately after the new Governor had assumed the 
duties of his office, and while Hasbeya was yet in the oceupation of the Govern. 
ment troops. 

flis Excellency having readily aceeded to the foregoing proposals, he 
directed his Kahyia in my presence to put them forthwith into execution in 
concert with the dragoman of the Consulate; and Emir Halil and Mahommed 
Keiss have already left, in consequence, Damascus,—the former for Hasbeya 
with troops and with the converts that Were in this city, and the latter for Le- 
hanon to accomplish his mission, and to deliver the Pasha’s latter (Inclosure 
No, 3) to the poor fugitives, 

I may venture, therefore, to announce very respectfully to your Lordship 
the present favourable termination of this question, which, after ending for 
several months, was in danger of becoming more and more complicated trom 
the unabating intrigues of all those parties whose religious sentiments or poli- 
tical views rendered them averse to the introduction of i purer faith; and 
which, if promulgated, cannot fail to give Her Majesty's Government a perma- 
nent influence in Syria. It is the knowledge of this fact that does not and will 
not permit the Russian Consul-General to leave undisturbed the arrangements 
made, be it by his opposition here or through the representations of the Russian 
Embassy to the Sublime Porte,

Halil Pasha to Aali Pasha. 


(After the customary Compliments.) 


SOME of the Christian Rayahs of Hasbeya having embraced the Pra- 
testant religion through the medium of designing men, the greater part of 
them have been made to return to their former ereed by the Patriareh, who con- 
veyed to them his advice through some of the notables of that district, Actually 
only twenty of them pretend to be Protestants ; but as it has been reported that 
your Excellency intended to punish those who had used. thelr utmost etforts to 
bring about their return to their Church, and had already ordered their impri- 
sonment aud punishment, they labour naturally under great fear and appre- 
hension, 

This has filled me with great astonishment, as those who have undertaken to 
cause their return to their Church deserved in return encouragement and mild- 
ness. It may be, however, that the report has been put in circulation by some 
evil-doers with the yiew of intimidating the Rayahs, and of preventing them 
thereby to aid in the recantation of the seceders, 


I have deemed it, therefore, expedient to write to you by way of suggestion, 


5] 


that you may be pleased to give such orders as may tranquillize and reassure the 
Rayahs, and'rectify and remove the above reports; at the same time that those 
who put them in circulation should be found and punished. You ought to 
countenance the Patriarch and those who are working for their return, that they 
may thus have the means of doing it more effectually,

religious affairs of his nation, 

I have now learnt with surprise and amazement that the notables of Has- 
heya, who have not given themselves up to foreigners, are menaced with 
imprisonment. It is my conviction that the object of it is to humble and 
degrade them, and to embolden those whoare with the missionaries, No doubt

(Translation,) October 2, 1844, 


WE have issued this our order, worthy of obedience to all » 
fled from Hasbeya and are now residing at the Village of Abeyio, fhe i ne 
know that it having come to our knowledge at the present date that you have 


a2 


abandoned your native place of Hasbeya, and have taken up your abode in 
Abeyie, we were greatly surprised at it; for the tranquillity and well-being of 
the Rayahs forms one of the especial Imperial commands and wishes: we have, 
in consequence, addressed you this our ordinance by the hand of one of the 
noblest sheiks, Sheik Mahommed Keiss, may his glory increase, that ye may, on 
his arrival and on your being made acquainted with it, expel from your minds 
all doubt and apprehension, and remain tranquil and reassured, and return 
forthwith to your native place, and assume in full repose and peace of mind 
your occupations and vocations, 

Be not troubled or perplexed on any account whatever, but return imme- 
diately to Hasbeya with the aforestated Sheik Mahommed Keiss, and with the 
grace of the Almighty ye will experience all'that is pleasing to you of tranquil- 
lity and repose of mind in your country. Do not retard.an hour your return to 
your homes. Know ye this and pay ye to it the greatest attention,

No. 87. 
The Earl of Aberdeen io Consul-General Rose. 
(No. 14.) 
Sir, Foreign Office, November 16, 1844. 


HER Majesty’s Government have taken into consideration your despatch 
No. 42, of the 7th of October, with its inclosures, with reference to the question 
which had arisen between the Consulates of Great Britain, Russia, gad Pruseie 
in Syria, relative to the persecution of certain Christian Rayahs in the Hasbeya, 
who had gone over from the Greek to the Protestant persuasion, 

_ Her Majesty's Government approve, generally, your conduct, as well as 
that of Mr. Wood in this matter; that is, they approve your having interposed 
to protect Christians from open persecution on the part of the Turkish autho- 
rities: and they also approve your having distinctly disclaimed all idea of 
interfering to protect those Christians as converts, with which matters Her 
Majesty's servants in the Levant have nothing to do. 

Her Majesty's Government consider that you acted rightly in not entering 
into a discussion with the Russian Consul-General on the point of the right 
assumed by him under the alleged authority of the Treaty of Kainardji, to 
stand forth, on the part of Russia, as the protector of the Greek Chureh, Her 
Majesty's Government think it highly inexpedient that any question should be 
raised upon it between the British and Russian Consulates in Syria, 

It is with much regret that Her Majesty's Government have seen the 
ferment which has been excited by the conversions which have been unwisely 
encouraged in the Hasbeya from the Greck faith; since such proceedings could 
scarcely fail to provoke the interference of Russia, as the virtual head of that 
faith; an interference on every account to be deprecated. 

It is with equal regret that we have observed the part which the Prussiau 
Consul-General has taken in these matters, and we consider his mention of 
Great Britain in the letter which he was unfortunately induced to address to 
the Emir Saad-el-Deen, uncalled-for and improper. 

The less interference is exercised henceforward in these questions on the 
part of the foreign Consulates in Syria, the better will it be, in the opinion of 
Her Majesty's Government, both for the country and for the Consulates; and 
the more certainly will the latter be enabled to support that just and useful 
influence which, when properly applied, may, in the present dubious state of 
Syria, be exerted with so much benefit to the country. 

You will have already received my despatch No, 10, of the 19th of Se 
tember, in which the line you are to pursue with respect to interference in 
relicious matters is clearly pointed out, ‘To the instructions contained in that 
despateh you will rigidly adhere; and in abstaining as much as possible from’ 
interference yourself, you will employ your best efforts to induce your colleagues 
to observe an equal abstinence on their part. 


53 


You will explain to Mr, Consul Wood, to whom I addressed in September 
last a despatch to the same effect as my despatch to you, No. 10, the viewsof 
Her Majesty's Government upon the points adyerted to in my present 
instruction, 


Tam, &c. 
(Signed) ABERDEEN.

in the Hasbeya, and the improper use which the Consul of Prussia with reference

I have, in consequence, addressed to Colonel Rose for his guidance, and

No, 39. 
Consul-General Rose to the Earl of Aberdeen.—(Received December 5.) 
(No, 45.) 
My Lord, Beyrout, October 28, 1544, 


[ HAVE the honour to acknowledge the receipt of your Lordship’s 
despatch, No, 10, and beg to say with great respect, that I shall strictly obey 
the instructions which it contains. | 

I trust that I may be permitted to observe that the prudent and just 
views expressed in that despatch, provide fully for the two matters to be dealt 
with, that is, that the faith of other sects should not be interfered with, and, 
secondly, that the rights of humanity should be protected in countries where 
unfortunately they are only too often trespassed on. 

When the conversions at Hasbeya were first talked of here, the circumstance 
that the dissidents had first addressed themselves to Bishop Alexander, and 
that ignorant people in this country confound American with British subjects 
on account of the similarity of language and faith, induced me to make 
communications in the sense of your Lordship’s confidential and separate 
instructions of the 27th November, 1841, indeed of your Lordship’s present 
instructions, to some of my colleagues and such other parties, whose attention 
might have been awakened by the report that British subjects were concerned 
in the conversions at Hasbeya, This I already have had the honour to report 
to your Lordship. 

Linformed these parties not only that British subjects had had nothing 
whatever to do with those matters, but that moreover as far as myself or Her 
Majesty's servants were concerned, they, Hritish subjects, would never interfere 
in any way with the religious belief of any faith in Syria, I instanced in proof 
of this that Bishop Alexander had refused to enter into any communication 
with the dissidents, when they expressed a wish to become converts to his 
Church. lL also ndded, in further proof of the strict system of non-intervention 
observed by British agents, that some of the Hasbeya converts had presented 
themselves to Mr. Consul Moore’s'Cancellier at the Consular Olfice, before they 
addressed themselves: to the American Missionaries, and declared their wish to 
become members of the Anglican Church; and | observed that this person was 
so well aware of the system followed by Mr. Moore and myself in these matters, 


* Ineclosure 1 in No. 34. 


3) 


ee ee 


54 


and of the positive refusal which we had always given to listen to or entertain 
similar requests, that without even consulting either Mr. Moore or myself, he 
had rejected the application of the Hasbheya converts. 

I also told Assaad Pasha in writing, that no British subject was concerned 
in the conversions at Hasbeya; that on the contrary, Bishop Alexander had 
rejected the application of some Greeks to be members of the Protestant faith ; 
and my letter to the Rev. Mr. Smith, which was so clear on this point, I have 
already had the honour to submit to your Lordship. ; 

'T'o the Sheiks of the converts, who with the American Missionary, the Rev. 
Mr. Thomson, and two or three of his co-religionists, visited me at Drumana: 
I stated explicitly, that any interest which had been used or would be used 
in their favour by Mr. Wood or myself, was solely to be attributed to the 
oppression of hint they had been the victims, of which oppression one unde- 
niable proof was, that they stood before me fugitives from their native town, 
without resourees, and separated from their wives, children, and relatives; but 
that neither Mr. Wood nor myself had anything whatever to do with the con- 
version, and could not enter into any discussion or conversation with them (the 
converts) on that matter, or any question of dogma or change of belief; that 
Her Majesty's servants having advocated the rights of humanity in favour of 
Christians of all sects, Jews, Mahommedans, Druses, and Anzariés, we felt that we 
could not refuse the same measure of benevolent assistance to such Christians 
as had been oppressed at Hasbeya; finally, that oppression having once ceased, 
all advocacy of them would cease also, ee 

Generally speaking, I cannot altogether approve the spirit which actuates 
the American Missionaries. ‘They rather lean to a theory of a sect to which 
they profess great repugnance, 

They follow a course which they secretly wish and expect will produce a 
certain result, although they publicly disavow that their object is to cause that 
result. ‘Thus they allay the fears of fathers and mothers of other Christian 
sects, by declaring that they do not seek to conyert, that their only object is to 
instruct, although they know and hope that the course of instruction which 
they give will induce their scholars to leave their faith, and be converted to 
theirs. Thus the child, when his education is completed, finds himself a semi 
perhaps a whole Protestant, the missionary, as before, declaring that he has no 
intention to make him so, but still gathering the fruit of which he had sown 
the seed, 

The result is, that as the missionaries, adhering to their system of not 
openly attempting to convert, teach only enough to awaken the attention of 
the student, and to shake his faith in bis own creed, but do not inculcate the 
truths of their own with fervour, at the end of an American education the 
Syrian finds himself a bad Greek or Maronite, and an indifferent Protestant, 
his ideas enlightened enough to make him alive to the errors of his faith, but 
not matured enough either to make him leave his old, or cling to his new belief 
with resolution, 

Again, the missionaries declare that they take no part whatever in politics, 
still | must confess that I have received communications, and heard hazardous 
wishes in critieal times which do not accord with so prudent a resolution. 

But having said so much, it is my duty to add that the American Mission- 
aries are generally men of education, and universally persons of good conduct. 

As revards the conversions at Hasbeya, it is only fair to say that, although 
ihe American Missionaries had formerly a school at Hasbeya, and had distri- 
buted books to its inhabitants, yet the declaration of the conversion appears to 
have been entirely on the part of the inhabitants, For they sought first Bishop 
Alexander, and then the American Missionaries. 

But it cannot be denied that much diseord and family dissensions have 
resulted from the anomalous system pursued by the American Mission. 


T have, &e. 
(Signed) HUGH ROSE.

Inclosure in No. 50. 
Consul Wood to Sir Stratford Canning. 


Sir, Damascus, Deceniber 10, 1844. 


* ‘ z 
* the honour to state that, im consequence of the secret commu- 
Kea de orang oe hy the authorities of Damascus relative to the Reap 
of Hasbeya and the Emir Halil, it was agreed that a Commission shou 0 : 
named to aseertain on the spot the exact wishes of the rv al yarties in regard 
to their Governor with the view to the adoption of such other ulterior measures 
as would satisfy them and appease their mutual irritation, . meen 

‘After the departure of the Commissioner, however, ant Brean: forte 
receipt of their report, Halil Pasha renewed his remonstrances an reals i 4 
letter which he ac dressed to Aali Pasha, wherein he insisted on the recall 'o 
Emir [lalil, and the appomtment of a Turkish officer tn his room, — _ 

His Hixcellency was pleased, in acquainting me with this fresh em tbe 
ment, to express a request that £ would give him my opinion with rega ie 
the line of conduct he should pursue, or at any rate, that I wane I .. him 
from so disagreeable an alternative as that of either aupersedling t 16: sanit, 
coutrary to his former eee i me, or of positively refusing to execute 
| + Porte’s Plenipotentiary. i 
as Cty with she above inessage, the receipt of a confidential 


communication from Her Majesty's Consul-General that the Russian Consul 


inti is intention of wi ing his co-operation in carrying through 
d intimated his intention of wit sholding his co-operatio ough 
the ntan adopted in 1842 for the administration o Mount Lebanon, until he 


63 


could ascertain anew the views of his Government, which the subsequent 
oeenrrence of the affairs of Hasbeya might have altered, on the assumption 
that they were connected with the former question ; cou led with my anxiety 
to avoid all complications, once that the main object ot bringing back the 
fugitive converts to their homes had been accomplished, as well as my intimate 
conviction that I could not better serve the interests of Emir Halil than by 
availing myself of the opportunity to preerre the Government of Hasbeya 
in his family, by causing it to be transferred to one of its members instead of 
a stranger, induced me, with other equally cogent considerations, to comply 
at once with the request of his Excellency. 

I therefore replied, that with regard to the nomination of a Turkish 
officer, [should abstain from giving any opinion, as I wished to be free to act 
according to circumstances, should they render my intervention necessar 
hereafter; for, as I felt certain that without a strong force to support his 
authority (and which would consume ihe revenues of the district) i must 
necessarily side with the strongest party to maintain himself, I would not 
become the indirect means of oppression to the weaker, which alone deserved 
our sympathy; but that I had no objection to offer to the appointment of an 
Emir, particularly should his Excellency’s thoice fall on Emir Ahmet, the 
nephew of Emir Halil. 

The Pasha adopted forthwith my suggestion in favour of the former Emir, 
and thus not only a proper reo has been shown to his wishes, but every pre- 
text upon which the Russian Consul could ground his refusal to co-operate with 
Her Majesty's Consul-General in the adjustment of the question of Lebanon 
has been removed ; and the poliey of the Porte’s Commissioner to substitute a 
Turk for a native Prince paralyzed; ‘at the same time that this Consulate has 
not lost any of the advantages which it necessarily derives from the knowledge 
of the Governor that he owes his nomination to its recommendation ; and which 
may iutflice, ina manner, to preclude the further necessity of its formal interven- 
tion in the affair, although it is evident from the information just received 
that the position of the converts is very far from being a satisfactory or a 
secure one. é 

As the GueMon, from its nature and long duration, has afforded ampic 
means whereby to judge of the feelings and policy of the different partics that 
have taken a greater or a less interest in it, [ may venture, very respectfully, to 
note them briefly. 

'The eagerness, which the Capudan Pasha has shown to seize the opportu- 
nity of nominating a Turkish Officer in the place of a native Prince, proves 
sufficiently that the Porte still adheres to its secret policy of establishing its 
direct rule by the substitution of its own for native Governors ; and the facility 
with which the Russian Consul-General acquiesced in it, that his Government 
is not averse to the system. 

Hut whenever it has been acted upon, experience has fully demonstrated 
that, instead of the natives being benefitted by it, it has genera ly led to their 
ruin, to anarchy and confusion, and to the consequent dilapidation of the public 
revenues, be it on account of the insatiable corruption or incapacity of the 
‘Turka, who cannot be expected besides, to take the same interest, as their own 
Emirs and Sheiks, in their well-being and prosperity. 

Although this fact cannot have escaped the penetration of the Hussian 
agenta, yet as it is easier for them to exorcise greater influence with the Turks 
than with the native Chiefs, who are more independent, they allow their own 
gpecial interests to govern their conduct at the aacrifice of the happiness of the 

ople. And it is perhaps in this apparent similitude of policy that may be 
ound the secret spring of Russian influence even with the higher functionaries 
of the Porte, who, unable to divine their real object, only see in it a friendly 
disposition to support them in their own. 

A similarity of views leads the Greek clergy to adopt it likewise and to go 
even heyond it; for what they are unable to accomplish with the native Chiets, 
they seldom fail in doing with the Turks in virtue of their Borats, which give 
them extensive powers and authority. In the instance of Hasbeya, by merely 
intimidating the Emir through the Pasha, they were able to establish a 
Meshika or a Government of élective Sheika, who armed and organized 
the people as a counterpoise to the constituted local authority: and as nine 
other districts, situated like Hasbeya, have imitated their example, it follows 


35 


G4 


that, through their support, the elements of future opposition and resistance ave 
being formed, while the Porte’s functionaries only see In ‘their zealous efforts 
to aid them in submitting their own for native Governors, a blind devotion to 
their interests and a dutiful compliance with their wishes, 

The power, which the success of the foregoing plan will give the clergy 
over the Greek population, is very great; and as they are under the immediate 
control of the Russian agents, they only will derive solid advantages from ue 
propagation, inasmuch as they will exercise paramount influence over the who e. 
Still, it cannot be denied that this eradual undermining of ‘the Porte’s 
authority is no less prejudicial to the Turkish Government than 1f 18 to the 
people, which naturally suffer from the very disorders they create, The Has- 
beyans, for instance, not only refuse actually to pay some items that form a 
part of their tribute, but insist also that their Governor shall merely execute 
the sentences of the judge whom they ma, elect; and have otherwise sae 
matters to such extremities, as to have ob: ized the Pasha of Damascus to sen 
troops to enforee the payment of the taxes, and to summon the most unruly 
to appear, in order to meet the accusations preferred against them by the Emir 
Soad-el-Deen, whom they have obliged to take refuge In this city, | 

This state of things is partly attributable to the Capudan Pasua, whose 
opposition to us is best explained by his well known predilections for the Russian 
(Government, and which incline him to favour the views of its agents. Should 
the present Ottoman ministers therefore ever appomt him permanently mn tlds 
country, to remove him from the cn ital, whieh is not improbable, it is to be 
feared a reaction against British influence will be the immediate consequence ; 
hesides the latitude which his presence will give to the Greek clergy, to carry 
out their plan of establishing Meshikas to the exclusive benefit of the 
Power which protects them. Pas 

The question of Hasbeya, with all its inconveniences and embarrassments, 
has still afforded a test whereby to judge not only of the extent of Gritish 
‘ufluence when exercised, but of the influence, policy, and feelings of the parties 
that thought themselves called upon to interfere, ‘The Porte’s Commissioner, 
the Greek Patriarch, and the Russian Consul-General, coalesced to put a stop 
to the propagation of Protestantism in Syria, the former, perhaps, out of 
deference, in part, to the wishes of the latter, whose policy has always heen to 
destroy every element of legitimate British influence, without stopping to con- 
sider of the propricty of the means to which he resorted for the accomplishment 

his object. 

" Ea eens agents, although they have taken a less decided part in 
it, have, nevertheless, scare ly allowed an opportunity to escape them to 
impress the authorities here with the danger to the Porte from the conversion 
of its Rayahs to the tenets of the Anglican church ; and the French Chargé- 
WAffuires at Constantinople has but very recently written to his Consul to 
demand further details on the matter; while those of Austria have, as yet, 
confined themselves to mere observations. ; 

On the other hand, the Turkish authorities at Damascus have’ steadfastly 
maintained, notwithstanding the poner remonstrances, and censure, and 
threats of their colleagues, the more humane and enlightened principle that all 
their Rayahs had an equal right to their protection, whatever might he their 
relicious opinions; and have never ce sed to respect the wishes of Her Majesty's 
agents on this essential point, and to be even guided by them in the selection 
of the Governors that circumstances rendered advisable to appoint from time to 
time to that distracted district. | 

T have, &e. | 
(Signed) RICHARD WOOD.

With reference to your despatch No. 40 of the 19th of December, I have 
ta acquaint you that in continuing, as you appear to have done, your interfer-

sions in that district, you have saad entirely contrary to the wishes of Her 
Majesty's Government, as explained to you in the first instance in my despatch 
No. 3 of the 19th of September last, and repeated in subsequent instructions 
from myself and from Colonel Rose (which last, however, could not have 
reached you when you acted, as in your despatch No. 40 you report yourself to 
to have done). Ihave now to repeat. my injunction that you should abstain 
for the future from all interference whatever in questions connected with the 
operations of any missionary societies for the conversion to any form of worship 
of the inhabitants of the district in which you reside.

Consul-General Rose to the Earl of Aberdeen.—(Received March 7.) 
(No. 12,) 


My Lord, Beyrout, February 10, 1845. 


| HAVE the honour to acknowledge the receipt of your Lordship’s 
despatch. No. 1, with its inclosure, a copy of your Lordship's despatch No. 6, 
to Mr, Consul Wood. 

I have the honour to say, that in October last, M. Basily read to me a 
letter, the same, as near as I recollect, as the letter sent to your Lordship by 
the Russian Government, 

I observed to M. Basily, that I had heard nothing of such a letter from 
Mr. Wood, that it had no signature, and was: therefore utterly unauthenticated 
and valueless. 

M. Basily would not say from whom or from where he had received it. 

T observed to M. Basily that no person was safe, if a letter without sig- 
nature, the contents of which were prejudicial to him, was to be attributed to 
and brought forward against him; that, therefore, | must consider that the letter 
which reflected on Mr. Wood as not genuine till it was proved to be so, 

And I felt it the more necessary to act thus by Mr. Wood, as M. Basily, 
also grounding his complaint on a letter without a signature, had not long 
before accused to me Mr, Wood of improper interference in writing a letter to 
the Pasha of Damascus, and charges which Mr. Wood entirely disproved on 
my informing him of the accusation, 

I also communicated with Mr. Wood on the subject of M. Basily’s accu- 
sation against him relatively to the letter, inclosure in your Lordship's 
despatch No. 6, to Mr. Wood, when he replied, “I never remember to have 
written to Emir Saad-el-Deen in the sense he (M. Basily) pretends, but I 
will look over the correspondence. Iam certain I never threatened to banish 
him (the Emir Saad-cl-Deen,) though I have often warned him confidentially 
of the consequences of his conduct.” And again, “If M. Basily attacks me 
in consequence of my letter to Emir Saad-el-Decn, he ought to produce it, 
otherwise it must be considered as utterly unauthenticated,” | 

5 


66 


Taking this into consideration, as well ag that M, Basily never offered 
any proof whatever of the genuineness of the letter which he attributed to Mr 
Wood, 1 took no further steps in the matter. 

It is also my duty to observe to your Lordship, that M. Basily, long 
before the affair of Hasbeya, had given evidence of much malevolence towards 
Nr, Wood, both to iat and to others on several occasions, indulging m 
assertions which were certainly unwarrantable. 

By a communication from Mr, Wood, I find that he has not received your 
Doreen despatch No. G. 1 have sent him, therefore, a copy of your Lord- 
ship's despatch No. 6, but there has not been time for him to answer it. 
trust, therefore, that your Lordship will be pleased to excuse Mr. Wood's 


silence. 
T have, &c. 
(Signed) HUGH ROSE.

The Karl of Aberdeen to Sir Stratford Canning. 
(No. 32.) 
Bir, Foreign Office, March 20, 1840. 


THE memorial, of which the inclosed ts a copy, Was presented to me the 
day before yeaa by @ numerous deputation, and is signed in the onzinal 
by the Archbishop of Canterbury and above fifteen thousand other persons of 
various stations in society. ou 

The object of it, as your Excellency will perceive, is to induce Her Ma- 
jesty’s Government to renew their exertions with the Porte to obtain a firman 
from the Sultan authorizing the completion of the building designed for a 
Protestant Church at Jerusalem. 

The last report which I received from your Excellency on this subject is 
contained in your despatch No, 85 of the rd of May of last year, and from 
that despateh it appeared that the consent of the Turkish Government to the 
resumption of the works would depend on the report which the Pasha of Saida 
had been called upon to furnish with reference to the buildings proposed to be 
erected at Jerusalem for the accommodation of the British and Prussian Con- 
sulates, among which the chapel was to be included. 
 Thave to desire that your Excellency will now ascertain from the Turkish 
Government whether the report in question has been received from Syria, ani 
the course which in that ease the Porte is prepared to take on this matter. 

In the event of any further hesitation beg shown by the Porte to grant 
the necessary permission for the resumption and completion of the works, your 

Exeelloney will call the attention of the Turkish Ministers to the inclosed 
memorial, and take such further measures a5 may appear to you best calculated 
for giving effect to the wishes expressed in it, ; 

You will at the same time express the earnest hope of Her Majesty’ 
Government that no further impediment may be opposed to the completion of 
of the buildings, and that the Porte will no longer object to grant the forma! 
sanction of a tirman for that purpoce, 


Tam, &c. 
(Signed) ABERDEEN,

Inclosure in No, 54, 


Memorial respecting Protestant Church at Jerusalem. 


To the Right Honourable the Earl of Aberdeen, K. T., &e., &e., &e., Her 
Majesty's Principal Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs, 
_, The Memorial of the Undersigned Members and Friends of the London 8o- 
ciety for Promoting Christianity amongst the Jews 
Sheweth, =, 
THAT it is with feelings of painful regret, that this Society are 


67 


compelled to address your Lordship upon a subject deeply affecting the well- 
being of the Church of England in the Teast, as well as her honour and digmity 
before the Oriental churches, and in the eyes of at | | 
That this Society was established in one thousand eight hundred and nine, 
for the purpose of spreading the gospel amongst the Jewish people; that it isa 
Bociety composed of many thousand persons, with nearly one thousand auxiliary 
societies in Her Majesty’s dominions; that its funds amount to twenty-six thou- 
cand pounds per annum; that his Grace the Archbishop of Canterbury it 
patron, and most of the bishops of the Church of England and Ireland, as well 
as a large number of peers and members of Parliament vice-patrons and vice 
presidents. So long ago as in the year one thousand eight hundred and twenty 
the Society commenced a mission at Jerusalem, which has been invariably con- 
ducted with the strictest deference and obedience to the existing Government 
of the country, and continued, amidst many trinls and difficulties incident to 
the undertaking, to enjoy unmolested, both under the Ottoman and Egyptian 
Governments, for many years the rivilere of endeavouring to promote the 
spiritual and temporal welfare of od’s ancient people in that holy city. In 
the year one thousand eight hundred and thirty seven, the Society, in order 
to give greater stability and permanency to the mission, appointed at its 
head a clergyman, specially ordained for that office by the Lord Bishop of Lon- 
don, and associated with him a medical gentleman, whose professional services 
for the relief both of the suffering Jews and the inhabitants generally led to 
an increased intercourse and good feeling between the missionaries and all 
classes of people in Jerusalem. 

In the year one thousand eight hundred and forty one his Grace the Arch- 
bishop of Canterbury, with the license of Her Majesty and under the authority 
of the act of the filth Victoria, chapter six, consecrated the Reverend Michae 
Solomon Alexander, a Bishop of the United Church of England and Treland, to 
reside at Jerusalem, with spiritual jurisdiction over the English clergy and others 
‘n union with the Chureh, together with German clergymen ordained by the 
bishop, throughout Palestine, Syria, Chaldea, Egypt, and Abyssinia, his chiet 
missionary care being direeted to the conversion of the Jews to Christianity. 
For the purposes also of promoting education a collage was. to be established, 
under the superintendence of the bishop. This important step was taken at the 
suggestion and with the concurrence and hearty co-operation of His Majesty the 
King of Prussia, who contributed the munificent sum of fifteen thousand pounds 
towards the permanent income of the Thishop. 

Thus encouraged, both at home and abroad, by the highest patronage, the 
Society urged forward with ‘nereased exertions the erection of a church for the 
performance of divine service, and buildings for the accommodation of the Bishop 
and missionaries, which they had, at very considerable expense, & ready com- 
menced upon a piece of ground purehaser for the purpose. ‘The erection of a 
church has since acquired additional importance from the need of a place of 
worship for the numbers of English and foreign Protestants who now frequent 
the holy city. 

Having a letter commendatory from his Grace the Arehbishop of Canter- 
bury, the Bishop of Jerusalem procecded—with the prayers and best wishes of 
the friends of Israel—to take charge of bis sacred trust, The reception he 
met with upon his arrival, which was insured by the marked countenance ancl 
protection afforded by Her Majesty's Government and the respect he has at all 
times and upon all oecasions received from the heads of other Christian 
Churches, a8 well as from the inhabitants of Jerusalem generally, consisting of 
so great a variety of differing sects, . the best evidence not only of the unobjec- 
tionable nature of the mission, but of the sound judgment an diseretion with 

which the holy functions of the Bishop have been exercised, ‘The valuable 
services of the eminent physician Doctor Macgowan, who accompanied the 
Bishop, his piety, professional skill, and compassionate feclings for suffering 
humanity, have greatly contributed to the harmony and kind feeling subsisting 
at this time between the missionaries and the people of Jerusalem. 

Taking all these circumstances ‘nto their consideration, the Society most 
deeply regrets that whilst the Greeks, Roman Catholics, Armenians, and other 

ninor sects of Christians, enjoy the permission to worshi God in their respec- 
tive temples, and whilst no privilege is withheld on the representations of 
French and Russian diplomacy, the pure-reformed religion of the British nation, 


68 


to whom under God Turkey is indebted for the recovery of Syria, should be 
alone proscribed, and her Protestant children alone denied the possession of a 
consecrated building for the service of God, and especially that recognition of 
the Protestant faith which is indispensable to insure protection, 

Satisfied that the impediments, from whatever cause or source they may 
have arisen, will be immediately and effectually overruled by your Lordship's 
interposition, through the agency of Her Majesty’s Ambassador to the Ottoman 
Government, and confident that your Lordship does not view with indifference 
an object enjoying such august patronage, so dear to many thousands of the 
members of the Church of England, and so important from its a bearing 
on Christian missions and its influence on the Churches of the East, and with 
the success of which foreign nations consider the dignity and interests of 
England to be so nearly connected, the Society earnestly entreat of your 
Lordship to send such instructions to that able nad distinguished representative 
of the British Crown, Sir Stratford Canning, as shall enable him to prefer the 
necessary representation of the case to the Turkish Government, and obtain 
from the Sultan a firman authorizing the completion of the buildings upon 
which so much money has been already expended, and which have excited such 
ceneral and intense interest. A strong proof of this interest is afforded by 
the fact that an English lady has undertaken to complete the church, and 
endow it with an income of one hundred pounds per annum for a permanent 
minister, as well as to contribute the sum necessary to form a fund for keeping 
the church in repair.

No. 57. 
Consul. Wood to the Earl of Aberdeen.—(Received April 4.) 


My Lord, Damascus, February 20, 1846. 

I HAVE the honour to receive, through the kindness of Her Majesty's 
Consul-General, copy of your Lordship's despatch No. 6 of the 20th of De- 
cember, inclosing copy of a letter presented to your Lordship by the Russian 
Minister by order of his Government, accompanied by a complaint against me 
as the writer of it for having unduly interfered in the matter of the conversions 
from the Greek faith to Protestantism, which have lately taken place in the 
district of Hasbeya; and with reference to which your Lordship is pleased to 
remark that as 1 had not sent a copy of it home, your Lordship was unable to 
judge whether it was genuine or not. 

Although I have not yet had the honour to receive'the original of your 
Lordship's despatch, I may, nevertheless, venture most pineal} to state in 
explanation, that in October last, I received a letter from the Emir Saad-el- 
Deen (a minute of which I have the honour to inelose herewith under No, 1) 
asking me to overlook what had occurred in. Hasbeya, to continue my friend- 
ship towards him and family, and thanking me for the nomination of his brother 
to pate him instead of a stranger. 

o this communication I thought it advisable to return a civil reply (inclo- 
sure No. 2) couched in terms of kindness, expressive of my concern at what had 
taken place, and advising him to assist his brother in putting an end to the mis- 
understanding that unhappily existed among the people for his own personal 
tranquillity and the prosperity of his house, which might be compromised, and 
which [ wished should be avoided, the more especially as 1 may not have it in 


‘my power to avert the evil consequences, considering the nature of my dutics 


or position, | 

In comparing my letter to the Emir with the copy sent by the Russian 
Consul to his Government it appeared to differ:— 

istly. In the general tone and spirit, mine being friendly, kind, and 
conciliatory, the copy being harsh, dictatorial, and menacing. 

2ndly. In the omission of an illusion made by me to certain rejoicing that 
had been made under the Prince’s palace. 

Srdly. Inthe original I conveyed the assurance to the Emir, that I had 
made use of my influence to get the Governorahip of Hasbeya transferred to his 
own. brother in order to obviate the injurious consequences that might pro- 
bably acerue to himself and family from the appointment of a stranger ( and 
which was the original intention of the authorities); whereas in the fictitious 
version it is distinctly stated that I had made use of my position to procure his 
deposition and removal. 

dthly. expressly mention in my letter that his brother and successor had 
pledged himself to put an end to the existing intrigues, and urge him (the 
Emir) in consequence to act in concert with him for the speedier attainment of 
so desirable an object, without making, however, the slightest allusion to either 
the Greeks or the converts, which is altogether omitted in M. Basily’s copy, 
notwithstanding that upon this my sincere recommendation and advice that an 
agreement should be affected between these two sects rests principally the whale 
tenor of my communication; and— 

Sthly. In the room of the anxiety manifested by me to. the Emir that he 
should enjoy peace and prosperity, a threat of banishment is held out in Mr. 
Basily’s copy, which the expression in it, “si yous faites le contraire vous enuseres 
léloiznement de votre famille de Hasheya,” seems to imply. 

And deeming that so great a difference as the omission of whole para- 
graphs, and the palpable change of others so as to convey a meaning opposed 
to the one intended or stated by me, could not have arisen from the mere care- 
lesaness of the translator; and above all, considering it impossible that by any 
mere inadvertance my own private letter, which takes fifty-four lines in the 
translation, could be reduced to twenty-six, not the half, which is the number 
of lines in the spurious copy, without an express intention to do so, I was induced, 
with a view of eliciting some explanation, or of coming to a satisfactory conclu- 


“sion thereon, to write a note (inclosure No, 3) to*the Emir, simply asking him

by attributing to me a. document written by a Greek peasant, who shared, in a. 
aes asily’s feelings as to ‘the, and who was influenced by him, 
(MM. Basily }, 


It is difficult to explain the motives of these proceedings for which I am 
not conscious there exists any manifest cause; and they are perhaps only 
referable to a secret’ wish or plan of M. Basily, to tender me less zealous in 
my humble efforts in the service of my country, by dint of constant and 
Suess intimidation exercised in the manner | have ventured to allude to 
above, 

But whatever his real motives may be, it forms no less a subject of very 

deep pain and regret to me that I should have been made the involuntary 
cause of extra trouble to your Lordship; and a source of sincere concern that 
any attempt should have been made to deprive me of your Lordship's favourable 
opinion, at a time when I was straining every nerve to give satisfaction to my 
superiors, and had made it my principal endeavour and most pleasing of duties 
to deserve your Lordship’s indulgent approbation, 
__ With regard to that part of the complaint which refers to my undue 
interference in the conversions from the Greck faith to Protestantism, I dare 
venture, very humbly, to state that, far from being privy to the separation of 
the Grecks from their chureh, T was not aware of the fact till after its occurrence 
ina Pashalic beyond the jurisdiction of my Consulate; and even then, with 
the view to appease the agitation both here and at Hasbeya, I declared formally 
to the Turkish authorities, that the converts were not entitled to British protec- 
tion, and used my utmost efforts, in the presence of the Rey, Mr. Allan, to 
dissuade the Rev. Mr, Smith from returning to that district where his presence 
could only add to the ferment that existed already, 

Tt was only when [became acquainted with the contents of the letter of 
the Porte’s Commissioner to the Pasha of Damascus, which tended to encourage 
the persecution of the seceders, already wanderers and fugitives, that deeming 
that the first Benepe of the “Tanzimat el Hairiyé” was infringed by the 
highest Turkish functionary in Syria, I claimed for them, from their own 
authorities, the enjoyment of the same protection which was granted to the rest 
of the Christian subjects of the Sultan. ‘This direct intervention occurred on 

the 25th of September, and immediately ceased, a few days after, on the receipt 
of your Lordship's instruction No. 3 of the 19th of the same month. My 
interference, up to that period was confined to friendly advice, be it to the 
Greeks, to the converts, or to the authorities. 

From the circumstance that the converts resided in Hasheya with one of 
the principal Emirs of which district Iwas ou the most friendly footing ever 
since 1840, to whom I had rendered valuable services, who twice owed his 
nomination to its Government to me, and whom I had saved from banishment 
to Adrianople duving the administration of Negib Pasha, who had procured 
from the Porte a firman to that effect, it was natural for me to communicate 
with him occasionally, thathe might know Iwas not. only aware of what was 
unhappily oecurring with his knowledge, but to remind him likewise of the non- 
fulfilment of his promises. In such cases, | wrote in the strictest confidence, 
and in the form of advice to the Emir, on whom | always urged the expediency, 
not of leaning to one party or the other, but of adjusting their differences, by 
removing the causes of their disagreement, I was led to adopt this course trom a 
sense of duty which made me feel that, being on very intimate terms with him, 
T ought not to neglect the right which my old acquaintance with him or his 
personal obligations to me gave me of using my influence with him for the good 
and peace of the country. And I am bound in justice to him to acknowledge 
that, until I ceased my communications with him lately, he was principally 
instrumental, whether in or out of office, owing to my unremitting representa- 
tions, in maintaining the tranquillity of Anti-Lebanon for the last three years. 

Finally, | may venture, with the utmost aubmission and deference, to plead 
as an excuse the peculiar and not unfrequently embarrassing position of Her 
Majesty's Consulate in Damascus, Without a single Consular Agent through- 
out this extensive Pashalic, Iam often often obliged to communicate directly 
in writing with the Governors of lle numerous districts which it comprises, either 

in reply to their letters or to procure information. On the other hand, cirewm- 
stances since 1851 having enabled me to acquire a knowledge of the country, 
and the nature of my duties in 1840 having necessarily brought me in contact

Inclosure 2 in No. 57. 


Consul Wood to Emir Saad-el-Deen. 
(Translation.) 


(After the customary Compliments.) Damascus, October 3, 1844. 

WE inform your Scigneury that we have lately received your esteemed 
favour of the lst instant, and we have duly understood all its contents, 

My beloved and respected friend, your attached friend has not in the least 
failed to offer you, both verbally and in writing, and through the writer of the 
present, in whose sincerity we know you rely, such advice and good counsel as 
tended to your interests and the continuance of your tranquillity ; but all this 
did not avail, but to contrary proceedings, in opposition to what you have been 
writing to us; so much so indeed that, even subsequently to your present 
letter, of which we now acknowledge the receipt, your promises remain unful- 
filled, since the very firing of musketry and other improper demonstrations took 
place under your very palace, 

This state of things has obliged us, conformably to a sense of our duties, 
to act against our personal wish; but still bearing in mind the means of pre- 
venting the ruin of your house, we withheld our assent to the appomtment of 
one of the greatest of your enemies, by causing it to be transferred to your 
brother and to no one else, in order to hinder any injury to you and to those 
belonging to you; since we did not wish to repay you in the manner you have 
acted towards us. Such being the case, your respected brother has been named 
to the Governorship of the Mukattaa, and has promised to ent off the existing 
intrigues and to prevent altogether the causes that give rise to them. 

f your Seigneury really wishes, as we also do, the continuance of the 
welfare of your house and of your own tranquillity, be one hand with your 
brother for the execution of his instructions in regard to the removal of the 
causes of intrigue and the better understanding of the Rayahs or people among 
themselves; for if the contrary were to occur, the result will be injurious and 
ruinous to your family, which we do not desire, but which it may not be in our 
power to remedy, considering the nature of our position and duties. 

On our own part, we wish that you should enjoy the utmost of pros erity 
and repose, and that nothing of what has happened should have occurred, see- 
ing the promise of your Seigneury to us when you were in Damascus, but which 
you appear to have forgotten on your return to your home. We are at a loss 
to understand how you preferred advice at variance with our own, until matters 
were brought to the present extremity. We hope, however, that heneeforward 
you will take into better consideration your own interests as regards the future; 
and trust, besides that, you will continue ORE pleasing correspondence that we 
may be satisfied of the state of your health, &e. 

T have, &c 


(Signed) SICHARD WOOD. 


Inclosure 3 in No. 57. 
Consul Wood to Emir Saad-el-Deen. 


(Translation,) Damascus, February 4, 1845. 
YOUR attached friend has just learnt that one of his friendly letters to you, 
according to the conditions of the amity that exists between us, written some 
time ago, has been gent to Beyrout, and has been made public there. We 
hope that your Seigneury will give us some detailed explanation concerning the 
manner in which this has occurred, how it was sent, mh by whom? Do excuse 
this trouble, &e. 

I have, &c. 

(Bigned) RICHARD WOOD,

Inclosure 4 in No. 57. 
Emir Saad-el-Deen to Consul Wood. 


(Translation.) 
(After the usual Compliments.) Damascus, February 14, 1846. 

WH have to inform your Seigneury that we have this moment received 
your note, the contents of which (concerning the intelligence which has reached 
you, that one of your former letters to us has been sent to Beyrout, and there 
made public,) we have understood, as well as your demand to know in detail 
how it has occurred, and everything respecting this affair. 

Beloved Sir! 1, your sincere friend, have never sent any of your letters, or 
copies of any of them, to Beyrout or elsewhere, It is true that, when the 
governorship of Hasheya wis civen to our brother Emir Halil, and some of our 
enemies attempted to ruin our house, we wrote to your Seigneury, conformably 
to the old friendship that exists between us, and to our conviction of your zeal 
for our interests and the welfare of our house, soliciting your nssistance in 
keeping off the evil from us. 

Some of the people about us saw your Seigneury’s reply to us; and we 
afterwards learnt from Hawadja Giorgios-el-Reis that when he went to Beyrout 
he wrote a copy of your letter in the manner he thought fit, not according to 
truth, which he gave to the Russian Consul-General there. After the return of 
the aforestated [individual] from Beyrout, we saw this copy, but did not find it to 
correspond with your letter. It is, perhaps, this transaction which has come to 
your Seigneury’s knowledge, Se, 

(L.8.) EMIR SAAD-EL-DEEN SHEHAB.

No, 60. 


Sir Stratford Canning to the Earl of Aberdeen —( Received June 5.) 
(No. 123.) 
My Lord, Constantinople, May 20, 1846, 

I HAVE long felt so deeply the failure of my exertions on behalf of the 
Protestant church at Jerusalem, that the receipt of your Lordship’s instruction, 
authorizing me to renew them in terms which imply a serious perseverance on the 
part of Her Majesty’s Government, afforded me the liveliest gratification ; nor 
should I have lost 2 moment in acting on that instruction, had I not thought that 
the delay of a few days would enable me to renew the subject with more advantage 
and better hope. At an interview which I had with the Turkish Minister for 
Foreign Affairs this morning, | communicated the substance and read the con- 
cluding passage of your Lordship’s instruction, endeavouring at the same time to 
convey to his Exeellency's mind a just idea of the very important memorial 
which accompanied it, and also of the painful impression which the Porte’s ill- 
crounded reluctance to comply with a request, as reasonable as it is natural, 
had of late produced in England. I must do Shekib Effendi the justice to say, 
that he received my communication with interest, and listened to my arguments 
with deference. He made no attempt to defeat my application by referrmg to 
the Pasha of Saida’s report, and requested that I would bring the matter under 
his more deliberate consideration in the form ofa note. With this invitation 
I propose to comply, and your Lordship may depend upon my 8} aring no pains 
to overcome whatever obstacles the Turkish Ministry or the authorities 
may still oppose to the grant of a a for building the intended edifice. 

have, &c. 
(Signed) STRATFORD CANNING. 


No. 61. 


Sir Stratford Canning to the Earl of Aberdeen.—{ Received July 23.) 


(No. 159). Confidential.) 
My Lord, Constantinople, Jtily 2, 1845. 

I HAVE taken the earliest opportunity, after the late interruption of 
diplomatic business at the Porte, to solicit a conclusive and satisfactory answer 


i 


to my application for a firman permitting the resumption of the suspended 
buildings, together with the erection of a Protestant chureh at Jerusalem, 
and I am happy to state that, although I am not et in possession of the 
Porte’s official assent, the terms in which Shekib E fendi expressed himself 
upon the subject at an interview which I had with his Excellency this morning, 
warrant a conviction that the wishes of Her Majesty's Government will be 
complied with, and that I shall receive an official communication to that effect 
in the course of a few weeks, The Turkish Ministers appear to have some 
unexplained motive for wishing to postpone the announcement of their com- 
pliance, and it is possible that other o tacles may arise; but I am satisfied 
that the present intention of the Porte is to grant the required firman, and IT 
trust that your Lordship will approve of my exercising a little further patience 
before I insist upon receiving a decisive official reply. 
T have, &c. 
(Signed) STRATFORD CANNING. 


No. 68. 


Sir Stratford Canning to the Earl of Aberdeen.—(Recewed September 24.) 
(Qo. 199.) 
My Lord, Constantinople, Auguat 25, 1845, 
A'Tan audience, which I had of the Sultan this morning, I delivered to His 
Highness the Queen's letter expressing Her Majesty's satisfaction at the con- 


76 


duct of Aali Effendi while Ottoman Ambassador in England. The Sultan, in 
reply, expressed himself very graciously with respect to Aali Effendi's services, 
and the importance which he attached to Her Majesty's approval of the manner 
in which they had been performed. His Highness took occasion to confirm 
what his Minister for Foreign Affairs had previously announced to me, namely, 
that he consented to issue an [mperial Firman for the completion of the Protes- 
tant church at Jerusalem and other suspended buildings with which that sacred 
edifice is connected. 1 have much pleasure in adding that His Highness parti- 
cularly requested me to represent this concession as @ mark of the cordial satis- 
faction which he felt in complying with Her Majesty’s wishes. 

It cannot but gratify your Lordship to learn that in everything which fell 
from the Sultan’s lips at this audience there was a marked expression of good- 
will towards the British Crown and nation, as well as of the most friendly can- 
sideration for Her Majesty's person, and confidence in the policy of Her 
Majesty's Government towards this Empire.

Sir Stratford Canning to the Earl of Aberdeen.—( Received October 5.) 
(No. 216.) 

My Lord, Constantinople, September 16, 18465. 

INCLOSED herewith is an English translation of the Sultan’s firman 
permitting the erection of a Protestant place of worship at Jerusalem. I have 
nddressed the original to Her Majesty's Consul in that city, and retain in this 
Embassy an exact copy of it legalized according to Turkish forms. 

Some delay, in addition to that which preceded the official communication 
of this important instrument, ling heen occasioned by the necessity of having 
the expressions corrected in one or two passages. 

Your Londstip. observe that according to the terms of the firman, it is 
understood that the church is to be situated in connexion with the Consular 
residence. 

This condition was anticipated in your Lordship’s instructions, and it may 
prove a real advantage ‘f it has the effect of placing the whole establishment 
more immediately under the control of Her Majesty's Government, whose 
wisdom Will determine the most advisa le mode of meeting the obligation which 
it si asi . . 

cannot in justice dismiss the subject without acknowledging the valuable 
assistance which I have received from Mr, Alison, and also from Mr. Frederic 
Pisani, in the long course of my endeavours to execute your Lordship's instrue- 
tions respecting it. 
I have, &e. 
(Signed) STRATFORD CANNING.

Tnclosure in No. 64. 


Firman addressed to the Walee of Saida, the Governor of Jerusalem, and others. 


(Translation.) 


IT has been represented both now and before, on the part of the British 
Embassy residing at my Court, that British and Prussian Protestant subjects 
visiting Jerusalem, mect with difficulties and obstructions, owing to their not 
jossessing a place of worship for the observance of Protestant rites, and it has 

een requested that ermission should be given to erect, for the first time, a 
sh Protestant place of worship within the British Consular residence at 
erusalem. 

Whereas it is in accordance with the perfect amity and cordial relations 
existing between the Government of Great Britain and my Sublime Porte, 
that the requests of that Government should be complied with as far as possible; 
and whereas, moreover, the aforesaid place of worship is to be within the 
Consular residence, my royal permission 18 therefore granted for the erection 
of the aforesaid speci pees of worship within the aforesaid Consular resi- 
dence, and my Imperia orders having been issued for that purpose, the 
Ses decree containing permission has been specially given from my 

mperial divan, 

When, therefore, it becomes known unto you, Walee of Saida, Governor 
of Jerusalem and others 3s aforesaid, that our royal permission has been 
granted for the erection, in the manner above stated, of the aforesaid place af 
worship, you will be careful that-no person do in any manner whatever oppose 
the erection of the aforesaid place of worship in the manner stated, and you 
will a act in contravention hereof; for which purpose my Imperial Firman 
is issued, 

(Qn its arrival, you will act in accordance with my Imperial firman isaued 
for this purpose, in the manner aforesaid. Be it thus known to you, giving 
full faith to the Imperial cypher. 

Written in the first day of the Ramazan, 1261. (eptember 10, 1845.)
